Almost Every Simply Typed λ \lambda -Term Has a Long β \beta -Reduction Sequence Thanks: \lsuper *The current affiliation is Tohoku University, Japan, and the mail address is asada@riec.tohoku.ac.jp
Kazuyuki Asada \rsuper a,*, Naoki Kobayashi \rsuper a, Ryoma Sin’ya \rsuper b, Takeshi Tsukada \rsuper a

Abstract
. It is well known that the length of a β \beta -reduction sequence of a simply typed λ \lambda -term of order k k can be huge; it is as large as k k -fold exponential in the size of the λ \lambda -term in the worst case. We consider the following relevant question about quantitative properties, instead of the worst case: how many simply typed λ \lambda -terms have very long reduction sequences? We provide a partial answer to this question, by showing that asymptotically almost every simply typed λ \lambda -term of order k k has a reduction sequence as long as ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -fold exponential in the term size, under the assumption that the arity of functions and the number of variables that may occur in every subterm are bounded above by a constant. To prove it, we have extended the infinite monkey theorem for words to a parameterized one for regular tree languages, which may be of independent interest. The work has been motivated by quantitative analysis of the complexity of higher-order model checking.



Jan. 14, 2018Feb. 22, 2019

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 15, Issue 1, Paper 16

1.
Introduction

It is well known that a
\beta
-reduction sequence of
a simply typed
\lambda
-term can be extremely long.
Beckmann
[
1
]
showed that, for any
k\geq 0
,

\max\{\beta(t)\mid t\text{ is a simply typed }\lambda\text{-term of order }k\text{ and size }n\}={\mathbf{exp}_{k}(\Theta(n))}

where
\beta(t)
is the maximum length of the
\beta
-reduction sequences
of the term
t
, and
{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(x)}
is defined by:
{\mathbf{exp}_{0}(x)}\triangleq x
and
{\mathbf{exp}_{k+1}(x)}\triangleq 2^{{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(x)}}
.
Indeed, the following order-
k
term
[
1
]
:

(\overline{2}_{k})^{n}\overline{2}_{k-1}\cdots\overline{2}_{2}(\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.a\,x\,x){((\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.x)c)},

where
\overline{2}_{j}
is the twice function
\lambda f^{\tau^{(j-1)}}.\lambda x^{\tau^{(j-2)}}.f(f\,x)

(with
\tau^{(j)}
being the order-
j
type defined by:
\tau^{(0)}=\mathtt{o}
and
\tau^{(j)}=\tau^{(j-1)}\to\tau^{(j-1)}
),
has a
\beta
-reduction sequence of length
{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(\Omega(n))}
.

Although the worst-case length of the longest
\beta
-reduction sequence is well known as above,
much is not known about the
average-case
length
of the longest
\beta
-reduction sequence:

how often
does one encounter a term having a very long
\beta
-reduction sequence?
In other words, suppose we pick a simply-typed
\lambda
-term
t
of order
k
and
size
n

randomly
; then
what is the probability that
t
has a
\beta
-reduction sequence longer than a certain bound, like

{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(cn)}
(where
c
is some constant)? One may expect that, although there exists
a term (such as the one above) whose reduction sequence is as long as
{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(\Omega(n))}
,
such a term is rarely encountered.

In the present paper, we provide a partial answer to the above question, by showing that
almost every simply typed
\lambda
-term of order
k
has a
\beta
-reduction
sequence as long as
(k-1)
-fold exponential in the term size, under
a certain assumption. More precisely, we shall
show:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(k,\iota,\xi)\mid\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k-1}(n^{p})}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(k,\iota,\xi)\right)}=1

for some constant
p>0
, where

\Lambda_{n}(k,\iota,\xi)
is the set of (
\alpha
-equivalence
classes
[-]_{\alpha}
of) simply-typed
\lambda
-terms
such that the term size is
n
, the order is up to
k
, the (internal)
arity is up to
\iota\geq k
and the number of variable names is up to

\xi
(see the next section for the precise definition).

Related problems have been studied in the context of
the quantitative analysis of

untyped

\lambda
-terms
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
.
For example, David et al.
[
2
]
have shown that
almost all untyped
\lambda
-terms are strongly normalizing, whereas the result
is opposite in the corresponding combinatory logic.
A more sophisticated analysis is, however, required in our case,
for considering only well-typed terms, and also for reasoning about
the
length
of a reduction sequence instead of a qualitative property like strong normalization.

To prove our main result above, we have extended the infinite monkey theorem
(a.k.a. “Borges’s theorem”
[
5
, p.61, Note I.35]
) to a parameterized version
for regular tree languages. The infinite monkey theorem states that for any word
w
,
a sufficiently long word almost surely contains
w
as a subword (see Section
2
for
a more precise statement). Our extended theorem, roughly speaking, states that,
for any regular tree grammar
\mathcal{G}

that satisfies a certain condition and any
family

(U_{m})_{m\in{\rm Nature}}
of
trees

(or tree contexts) generated by
\mathcal{G}
such that
|U_{m}|=\mathrm{O}(m)
,
a sufficiently large tree
T
generated by
\mathcal{G}
almost
surely contains
U_{\lceil p\log{|T|}\rceil}
as a subtree
(where
p
is some positive constant).
Our main result is then obtained by preparing a regular tree grammar for simply-typed
\lambda
-terms,
and using as
U_{m}
a term having a very long
\beta
-reduction sequence, like

(\overline{2}_{k})^{m}\overline{2}_{k-1}\cdots\overline{2}_{2}(\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.a\,x\,x)((\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.x)c)
given above.
The extended infinite monkey theorem mentioned above may be of independent interest and applicable to
other problems.

Our work is a part of our long-term project on the quantitative
analysis of the complexity of higher-order model checking
[
6
,
7
]
. The
higher-order model checking asks
whether the (possibly infinite) tree generated by a ground-type term
of the
\lambda
Y-calculus (or, a higher-order recursion scheme)
satisfies a given regular property, and it is known that the problem is

k
-EXPTIME complete for order-
k
terms
[
7
]
. Despite the huge worst-case
complexity, practical model checkers
[
8
,
9
,
10
]

have been built, which run fast for many typical inputs, and have successfully been
applied to automated verification of functional programs
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
.
The project aims to provide a theoretical justification for it, by studying
how many

inputs actually suffer from the worst-case complexity.
Since the problem appears to be hard due to recursion,
as an intermediate step towards the goal, we aimed to analyze the variant of the problem
considered by Terui
[
15
]
: given a term of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus (without recursion)
of type Bool, decide whether it evaluates to true or false (where Booleans are Church-encoded; see
[
15
]

for the precise definition). Terui has shown that even for the problem, the complexity is
k
-EXPTIME complete
for order-
(2k+2)
terms. If, contrary to the result of the present paper,
the upper-bound of the lengths of
\beta
-reduction sequences
were
small for almost every term,
then we could have concluded that the decision problem above is easily solvable for most of the inputs.
The result in the present paper does not necessarily provide a negative answer to the question above,
because one need not necessarily apply
\beta
-reductions to solve Terui’s decision problem.

The present work may also shed some light on other problems on typed
\lambda
-calculi
with exponential or higher worst-case complexity. For example,
despite DEXPTIME-completeness of ML typability
[
16
,
17
]
,
it is often said that the exponential behavior is rarely seen
in practice
. That is,
however, based on only empirical studies.
A variation of our technique may be used to provide a theoretical justification (or possibly
un
justification).

A preliminary version of this article appeared in Proceedings of FoSSaCS 2017
[
18
]
.
Compared with the conference version, we have strengthened the main result (from “almost every
\lambda
-term
of size
n
has
\beta
-reduction sequence as long as
\exp_{k-2}(n)
” to
“… as long as
\exp_{k-1}(n^{p})
”), and added proofs. We have also generalized the argument in
the conference version to formalize the parameterized infinite monkey theorem for regular tree languages.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
states our main result formally.
Section
3
proves the extended infinite monkey theorem, and
Section
4

proves the main result.
Section
5
discusses related work, and
Section
6
concludes this article.

2.
Main Results

This section states the main results of this article: the result on the quantitative analysis of
the length of
\beta
-reduction sequences of simply-typed
\lambda
-terms (Section
2.1
)
and the parameterized infinite monkey theorem for
regular tree grammars (Section
2.2
).
The latter is used in the proof of the former in Section
4
.
In the course of giving the main results, we also introduce various notations used
in later sections; the notations are summarized in Appendix
A
.
We assume some familiarity with the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus and
regular tree grammars and omit to define some standard concepts (such as
\beta
-reduction);
readers who are not familiar with them
may wish to consult
[
19
]
about the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus
and
[
20
,
21
]
about regular tree grammars.

For a set
A
, we denote by
\#\!\left(A\right)
the cardinality of
A
; by abuse of notation, we write
\#\!\left(A\right)=\infty
to mean that
A
is infinite.
For a sequence
s
, we also denote by
\#\!\left(s\right)
the length of
s
.
For a sequence
s=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{\#\!\left(s\right)}
and
i\leq\#\!\left(s\right)
,
we write
s{\centerdot}i
for the
i
-th element
a_{i}
. We write
s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
or just
s_{1}s_{2}

for the concatenation of two sequences
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.
We write
\epsilon
for the empty sequence.
We use
\biguplus
to denote the union of disjoint sets.
For a set
I
and a family of sets
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
, we define

\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}\triangleq\biguplus_{i\in I}(\{i\}\times A_{i})=\{(i,a)\mid i\in I,a\in A_{i}\}
.
For a map
f
, we denote the domain and image of
f
by
\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(f)
and
\mathrm{Im}\left(f\right)
, respectively.
We denote by
\log{x}
the binary logarithm
\log_{2}{x}
.

2.1.
The Main Result on Quantitative Analysis of the Length of
\beta
-Reductions

The set of (
simple
)
types
, ranged over by
\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots
,
is defined by the grammar:

\tau::=\mathtt{o}\mid\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}.

Note that the set of types can also be generated by the following grammar:

\tau::=\tau_{1}\to\cdots\to\tau_{k}\to\mathtt{o}

where
k\geq 0
; we sometimes use the latter grammar for inductive definitions of functions on types.
We also use
\sigma
as a metavariable for types.

Remark 1
.

We have only a single base type
\mathtt{o}
above. The main result
(Theorem
2
) would not change even if there are
multiple base types.

Let
V
be a countably infinite set, which is ranged over by
x,y,z\,(\text{and }x^{\prime},x_{1},\text{etc.})
.
The set of
\lambda
-terms
(or
terms
), ranged over by
t,t_{1},t_{2},\ldots
, is defined by:

\displaystyle t::=x\mid\lambda\overline{x}^{\tau}\!.t\mid t_{1}\,t_{2}\qquad\qquad\overline{x}::=x\mid{\ast}.

We call elements of
V\cup\{{\ast}\}

variables
, and use meta-variables

\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z}\,(\text{and }\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{x}_{1},\text{etc.})
for them.
We sometimes omit type annotations and just write
\lambda\overline{x}.t
for
\lambda\overline{x}^{\tau}\!.t
.
We call
the special variable
{\ast}
an
unused variable
, which may be
bound by
\lambda
, but must not occur in the body.
In our quantitative analysis below, we will count
the number of variable names occurring in a term, except
{\ast}
.
For example, the term
\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x
in the standard syntax can be represented by
\lambda x.\lambda{\ast}.\lambda{\ast}.x
,
and the number of used variables in the latter is counted as
1
.

Terms of our syntax can be translated to usual
\lambda
-terms by regarding
elements in
V\cup\{{\ast}\}
as usual variables.
Through this identification we define the notions of free variables, closed terms, and

\alpha
-equivalence
\sim_{\alpha}
.
The
\alpha
-equivalence class of a term
t
is written as
[t]_{\alpha}
.
In this article, we distinguish between a term and its
\alpha
-equivalence class, and
we always use
[-]_{\alpha}
explicitly.
For a term
t
, we write
\mathbf{FV}(t)
for the set of all the free variables of
t
.

For a term
t
,
we define the set
\mathbf{V}(t)
of variables (except
{\ast}
) in
t
by:

\mathbf{V}(x)\triangleq\{x\}\quad\mathbf{V}(\lambda x^{\tau}\!.t)\triangleq\{x\}\cup\mathbf{V}(t)\quad\mathbf{V}(\lambda{\ast}^{\tau}\!.t)\triangleq\mathbf{V}(t)\quad\mathbf{V}(t_{1}t_{2})\triangleq\mathbf{V}(t_{1})\cup\mathbf{V}(t_{2}).

Note that neither
\mathbf{V}(t)
nor even
\#\!\left(\mathbf{V}(t)\right)
is preserved by
\alpha
-equivalence.
For example,

t=\lambda x.(\lambda y.y)(\lambda z.x)

and

t^{\prime}=\lambda x.(\lambda x.x)(\lambda{\ast}.x)

are
\alpha
-equivalent, but
\#\!\left(\mathbf{V}(t)\right)=3
and
\#\!\left(\mathbf{V}(t^{\prime})\right)=1
.
We write
\mathtt{\#vars}{(t)}
for

\min_{t^{\prime}\in[t]_{\alpha}}\!\!\#\!\left(\mathbf{V}(t^{\prime})\right)
, i.e.,
the minimum number of variables required
to represent an
\alpha
-equivalent term.
For example, for
t=\lambda x.(\lambda y.y)(\lambda z.x)
above,

\mathtt{\#vars}{(t)}=1
,
because
t\sim_{\alpha}t^{\prime}
and

\#\!\left(\mathbf{V}(t^{\prime})\right)=1
.

A
type environment

\Gamma
is a finite set of type bindings of the form

x:\tau
such that if
(x:\tau),(x:\tau^{\prime})\in\Gamma
then
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
;
sometimes we regard an environment also as a function.
When we write
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, we implicitly require that

(x:\tau)\in\Gamma_{1}
and
(x:\tau^{\prime})\in\Gamma_{2}
imply
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
,
so that
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
is well formed.
Note that
({\ast}:\tau)
cannot belong to a type environment;
we do not need any type assumption for
{\ast}
since it does not
occur in terms.

The type judgment relation
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is inductively defined by the following typing rules.

\begin{gathered}\frac{}{x:\tau\vdash x:\tau}\qquad\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash t_{1}:\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\qquad\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{2}:\sigma}{\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{1}t_{2}:\tau}\\[10.0pt]
\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\tau\qquad\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\text{ or }\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\cup\{\overline{x}:\sigma\}\qquad\overline{x}\notin\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\overline{x}^{\sigma}\!.t:\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau}\end{gathered}

The type judgment relation is equivalent to the usual one for the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus, except that a
type environment for a term may contain only variables that occur free in the term
(i.e., if
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
then
\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{FV}(t)
).
Note that if
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is derivable, then the derivation is unique.

Below we consider only well-typed
\lambda
-terms, i.e., those that are inhabitants of some typings.

{defi}

[Size, Order and Internal Arity of a Term]
The
size
of a term
t
, written
|t|
, is defined by:

\displaystyle|x|\triangleq 1\;\;\;\;\;\;|\lambda\overline{x}^{\tau}\!.t|\triangleq|t|+1\;\;\;\;\;\;|t_{1}t_{2}|\triangleq|t_{1}|+|t_{2}|+1.

The
order
and
internal arity
of a type
\tau
, written

\mathtt{ord}(\tau)
and
\mathtt{iar}(\tau)
respectively, are defined by:

\displaystyle\mathtt{ord}(\tau_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\tau_{n}\rightarrow\mathtt{o})\triangleq\max(\{0\}\cup\{\mathtt{ord}(\tau_{i})+1\mid 1\leq i\leq n\})

\displaystyle\mathtt{iar}(\tau_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\tau_{n}\rightarrow\mathtt{o})\triangleq\max(\{n\}\cup\{\mathtt{iar}(\tau_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\})

where
n\geq 0
. We denote by
\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota)
the set of types
\{\tau\mid\mathtt{ord}(\tau)\leq\delta,\mathtt{iar}(\tau)\leq\iota\}
.
For a judgment

\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
,
we define
the
order
and
internal arity
of
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, written

\mathtt{ord}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)
and
\mathtt{iar}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)
respectively, by:

\displaystyle\mathtt{ord}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)\triangleq\max\{\mathtt{ord}(\tau^{\prime})\mid(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau^{\prime})\text{ occurs in }\Delta\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{iar}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)\triangleq\max\{\mathtt{iar}(\tau^{\prime})\mid(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau^{\prime})\text{ occurs in }\Delta\}

where
\Delta
is the (unique) derivation tree for
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
.

Note that the notions of size, order, internal arity, and
\beta(t)

(the maximum length of
\beta
-reduction sequences of
t
, as defined in Section
1
)
are well-defined with respect to

\alpha
-equivalence.

{exa}

Recall the term
\overline{2}_{j}\triangleq\lambda f^{\tau^{(j-1)}}.\lambda x^{\tau^{(j-2)}}.f(f\,x)
(with
\tau^{(j)}
being the order-
j
type defined by:
\tau^{(0)}=\mathtt{o}
and
\tau^{(j)}=\tau^{(j-1)}\to\tau^{(j-1)}
) in Section
1
.
For
t=\overline{2}_{3}\,\overline{2}_{2}\,(\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.x)\,y
,
we have
\mathtt{ord}(y\mathbin{:}\mathtt{o}\vdash t:\mathtt{o})=\mathtt{iar}(y\mathbin{:}\mathtt{o}\vdash t:\mathtt{o})=3
.
Note that the derivation for
y\mathbin{:}\mathtt{o}\vdash t:\mathtt{o}
contains the type judgment
\emptyset\vdash\overline{2}_{3}:\tau^{(3)}
, where
\tau^{(3)}=((\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o})\to(\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o}))\to(\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o})\to\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o}
, and

\mathtt{ord}(\tau^{(3)})=\mathtt{iar}(\tau^{(3)})=3
.

We now define the sets of (
\alpha
-equivalence classes of) terms
with bounds on the order, the internal arity, and the number of variables.

{defi}
[Terms with Bounds on Types and Variables]

Let
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
be integers.
For each
\Gamma
and
\tau
,

\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
and

\Lambda_{n}(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
are defined by:

\displaystyle\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{[t]_{\alpha}\mid\Gamma\vdash t:\tau,\mathtt{ord}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)\leq\delta,\mathtt{iar}(\Gamma\vdash t:\tau)\leq\iota,\mathtt{\#vars}{(t)}\leq\xi\}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid|t|=n\}.

We also define:

{\iota}{\xi}\triangleq\,\biguplus_{\mathclap{\tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota)}}\,\Lambda(\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\qquad\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\triangleq\{[t]_{\alpha}\in{\iota}{\xi}\mid|t|=n\}.

Intuitively,
\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)

is the set of (the equivalence classes of)
terms of type
\tau
under
\Gamma
, within
given bounds
\delta
,
\iota
, and
\xi
on the order, (internal) arity, and the number of variables respectively;
and
\Lambda_{n}(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
is the subset of

\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
consisting of terms of size
n
.
The set
{\iota}{\xi}
consists of (the equivalence classes of)
all the closed (well-typed) terms, and

\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)
consists of those of size
n
.
For example,

t=[\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.\lambda y^{\mathtt{o}}.\lambda z^{\mathtt{o}}.x]_{\alpha}
belongs to

\Lambda_{4}(1,3,1)
; note that

|t|=4
and

\mathtt{\#vars}{(t)}=\mathtt{\#vars}{(\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.\lambda{\ast}.\lambda{\ast}.x)}=1
.

We are now ready to state our main result, which is proved in Section
4
.

Theorem 2
.

For
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 2
and
k=\min\{\delta,\iota\}
, there exists a real number
p>0
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k-1}(n^{p})}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\right)}=1.

As we will see later (in Section
4
), the
denominator
\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\right)
is nonzero if
n
is sufficiently
large.
The theorem above says that if the order, internal arity, and the number of used variables are
bounded independently of term size, most of the simply-typed
\lambda
-terms of size
n
have
a very long
\beta
-reduction sequence, which is as long as

(k-1)
-fold exponential in
n
.

Remark 3
.

Recall that, when
k=\delta
, the worst-case length of
\beta
-reduction sequence is
k
-fold
exponential
[
1
]
.
We do not know whether
k-1
can be replaced with
k
in the theorem above.

Note that in the above theorem, the order
\delta
, the internal arity
\iota
and the number
\xi
of
variables are bounded above by a constant,
independently of the term size

n
. Our proof of the theorem
(given in Section
4
) makes use of this assumption to model the set of simply-typed
\lambda
-terms
as a regular tree language.
It is debatable whether our assumption is reasonable. A slight change of the assumption
may change the result, as is the case for strong normalization of untyped
\lambda
-terms
[
2
,
4
]
.
When
\lambda
-terms are viewed as models of functional programs,
our rationale behind the assumption is as follows.
The assumption that the size of types (hence also the order and the internal arity) is fixed
is sometimes assumed in the context of type-based program analysis
[
22
]
. The assumption on the number
of variables comes from the observation that a large program usually consists of a large number of

small
functions, and that the number of variables is bounded by the size of each function.

2.2.
Regular Tree Grammars and Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem

To prove Theorem
2
above,
we extend the well-known
infinite monkey theorem
(a.k.a. “Borges’s
theorem”
[
5
, p.61, Note I.35]
) to a parameterized version for regular tree grammars,
and apply it to the regular tree grammar that generates
the set of (
\alpha
-equivalence classes of) simply-typed

\lambda
-terms. Since the extended infinite monkey theorem may be of independent interest,
we state it (as Theorem
6
) in this section as one of the main results. The theorem is proved in Section
3
.
We first recall some basic definitions for regular tree grammars
in Sections
2.2.1
and
2.2.2
, and then state
the parameterized infinite monkey theorem in Section
2.2.3
.

2.2.1.
Trees and Tree Contexts

A
ranked alphabet

\Sigma
is a map from a finite set of
terminal
symbols
to the set of natural numbers. We use the metavariable
a
for a terminal,
and often write
\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b},\mathtt{c},\ldots
for concrete terminal symbols.
For a terminal
a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Sigma)
, we call
\Sigma(a)
the
rank

of
a
.
A
\Sigma
-tree
is a tree constructed from terminals in
\Sigma

according to their ranks:

a(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
is a
\Sigma
-tree if

T_{i}
is a
\Sigma
-tree for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,\Sigma(a)\}
.
Note that
\Sigma(a)
may be
0
:
a(\,)
is a
\Sigma
-tree if
\Sigma(a)=0
.
We often write just
a
for
a(\,)
.
For example, if
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{a}\mapsto 2,\mathtt{b}\mapsto 1,\mathtt{c}\mapsto 0\}
,
then
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{c})
is a tree; see (the lefthand side of) Figure
1
for
a graphical illustration of the tree.
We use the meta-variable
T
for trees.
The
size
of
T
,
written
|T|
, is the number of occurrences of terminals in
T
.
We denote the set of all
\Sigma
-trees by
{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
,
and the set of all
\Sigma
-trees of size
n
by
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

\Tree[[[].{$\mathtt{c}$}].{$\mathtt{b}$}[].{$\mathtt{c}$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}

\Tree[[[].{$[\,]$}[].{$\mathtt{c}$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}[[].{$[\,]$}].{$\mathtt{b}$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}

Figure 1
.
A tree
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{c})
(left) and a context

\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}([\,]))
(right).

Before introducing grammars, we define tree contexts,
which
play an important role in our formalization and proof of the (parameterized) infinite
monkey theorem for regular tree languages.
The set of
contexts
over a ranked alphabet
\Sigma
, ranged over by

C
, is defined by:

C::=[\,]\mid a(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\Sigma(a)}).

In other words, a context is a tree where the alphabet is extended
with the special nullary symbol
[\,]
. For example,
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}([\,]))

is a context over
\{\mathtt{a}\mapsto 2,\mathtt{b}\mapsto 1,\mathtt{c}\mapsto 0\}
;
see (the righthand side of) Figure
1
for
a graphical illustration.
We write
\mathcal{C}({\Sigma})
for the set of contexts over
\Sigma
.
For a context
C
,
we write
\mathtt{hn}(C)
for the number of occurrences of
[\,]
in
C
.
We call
C
a
k
-context

if
\mathtt{hn}(C)=k
,
and an
affine context
if
\mathtt{hn}(C)\leq 1
.
A 1-context is also called a
linear context
.
We use the metavariable
S
for linear contexts and
U
for affine contexts.
We write
[\,]_{i}
for the

i
-th hole occurrence (in the left-to-right order) of
C
.

For contexts
C
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}
,
we write

C[C_{1},\dots,C_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}]

for the context obtained by
replacing each
[\,]_{i}
in
C
with
C_{i}
. For example, if

C=\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{a}([\,],[\,]))
,

C_{1}=\mathtt{b}([\,])
,
C_{2}=\mathtt{c}
, and
C_{3}=[\,]
, then

C[C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}]=\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{c},[\,]))
;
see Figure
2
for a graphical illustration.
Also, for contexts
C
,
C^{\prime}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathtt{hn}(C)\}
,
we write
C[C^{\prime}]_{i}
for the context obtained by
replacing
[\,]_{i}
in
C
with
C^{\prime}
. For example, for
C
and
C_{1}
above,

C[C_{1}]_{2}=\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),[\,]))
.

For contexts
C
and
C^{\prime}
,
we say that
C
is a
subcontext of

C^{\prime}
and write
C\preceq C^{\prime}
if
there exist
C_{0}
,
C_{1},\ldots,C_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}
, and
1\leq i\leq\mathtt{hn}(C_{0})
such that
C^{\prime}=C_{0}[C[C_{1},\dots,C_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}]]_{i}
. For example,

C=\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),[\,])

is a subcontext of
C^{\prime}=\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c}))
, because

C^{\prime}=C_{0}[C[[\,],\mathtt{c}]]_{2}
for
C_{0}=\mathtt{a}([\,],[\,])
; see also
Figure
3
for a graphical illustration.
The subcontext relation
\preceq
may be regarded as a generalization
of the subword relation.
In fact, a word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{k}
can be viewed as a
linear context
w^{\sharp}=a_{1}(\cdots(a_{k}[\,])\cdots)
; and
if
w
is a subword of
w^{\prime}
, i.e., if

w^{\prime}=w_{1}ww_{2}
, then
w^{\prime\sharp}=w_{1}^{\sharp}[w^{\sharp}[w_{2}^{\sharp}]]
.

C:

\Tree[[].{$[\,]$}[[].{$[\,]$}[].{$[\,]$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}

C_{1}:

\Tree[[].{$[\,]$}].{$\mathtt{b}$}

C[C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}]:

\Tree[[[].{$[\,]$}].{$\mathtt{b}$}[[].{$\mathtt{c}$}[].{$[\,]$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}].{$\mathtt{a}$}

Figure 2
.
Context filling (where
C_{2}=c
and
C_{3}=[\,]
).

Figure 3
.
A graphical illustration of a subcontext. The part surrounded by
the rectangle is the subcontext
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),[\,])
.

The
size
of a context
U
, written
|U|
, is defined by:

|[\,]|\triangleq 0
and
|a(C_{1},\dots,C_{\Sigma(a)})|\triangleq 1+|C_{1}|+\dots+|C_{\Sigma(a)}|
.
Note that
[\,]
and 0-ary terminal
a
have different sizes:
|[\,]|=0
but
|a|=1
.
For a
0
-context
C
,
|C|
coincides with the size of
C
as a tree.

2.2.2.
Tree Grammars

A
regular tree grammar

[
20
,
21
]
(
grammar
for short) is a triple
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
where
(i)
\Sigma
is a ranked alphabet;
(ii)
{\mathcal{N}}
is a finite set of symbols called
nonterminals
;
(iii)
{\mathcal{R}}
is a finite set of
rewriting rules
of the form

N\longrightarrow C[N_{1},\dots,N_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}]
where

C\in\mathcal{C}({\Sigma})
and
N,N_{1},\dots,N_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}\in{\mathcal{N}}
.
We use the metavariable
N
for nonterminals.
We write
\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}}
for
the ranked alphabet
\Sigma\cup\{N\mapsto 0\mid N\in{\mathcal{N}}\}
and often regard the right-hand-side of a rule as a
(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
-tree.

The rewriting relation
\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}
on
{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
is inductively defined by the following rules:

\displaystyle\frac{(N\longrightarrow C[N_{1},\dots,N_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}])\in{\mathcal{R}}}{N\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}C[N_{1},\dots,N_{\mathtt{hn}(C)}]}\qquad\frac{T_{i}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}T^{\prime}_{i}}{a(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},T^{\prime}_{i},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{k})}

We write
\longrightarrow^{*}_{{\mathcal{G}}}
for the reflexive and transitive closure of
\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}
.
For a tree grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
and a nonterminal

N\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
the language
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
of

N
is defined by

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\triangleq\{T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)\mid N\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}T\}
.
We also define

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\triangleq\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid|T|=n\}
.
If
{\mathcal{G}}
is clear from the context, we often omit
{\mathcal{G}}
and
just write
T\longrightarrow^{*}T^{\prime}
,
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N\right)
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left(N\right)
for

T\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}T^{\prime}
,
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, respectively.

We are interested in not only trees, but also contexts generated by a grammar.
Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a grammar.
The set of
context type
s of
{\mathcal{G}}
, ranged over by
\kappa
,
is defined by:

\kappa::=N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N

where
k\in{\rm Nature}
and
N,N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}\in{\mathcal{N}}
. Intuitively,
N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N

denotes the type of
k
-contexts
C
such that

N\longrightarrow^{*}C[N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}]
.
Based on this intuition, we define
the sets
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)

and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
of contexts by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{C\in\mathcal{C}({\Sigma})\mid\mathtt{hn}(C)=k,N\longrightarrow^{*}_{{\mathcal{G}}}C[N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}]\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\mid|C|=n\}.

We also write
C\mathbin{:}\kappa
when
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
(assuming that
{\mathcal{G}}
is clear from the context).
Note that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},{\Rightarrow}N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, and
we identify a context type
{\Rightarrow}N
with the nonterminal
N
.
We call an element in
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
a
\kappa
-context
,
and also a
\kappa
-tree
if it is a tree.
It is clear that,
if
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)

and
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{i}_{1}\cdots N^{i}_{\ell_{i}}{\Rightarrow}N_{i}\right)
(
i=1,\dots,k
), then

C[C_{1},\dots,C_{k}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{1}_{1}\cdots N^{1}_{\ell_{1}}\cdots N^{k}_{1}\cdots N^{k}_{\ell_{k}}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
We also define:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\,\bigcup_{\mathclap{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}}\,{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\mid|T|=n\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\,\bigcup_{\mathclap{N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}}}\,{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{n}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\{S\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})\mid|S|=n\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\cup{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{n}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\{U\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{G}})\mid|U|=n\}.

{exa}

Consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}_{0}=(\{\mathtt{a}\mapsto 2,\mathtt{b}\mapsto 1,\mathtt{c}\mapsto 0\},\{A_{0},B_{0}\},{\mathcal{R}}_{0})
, where
{\mathcal{R}}_{0}

consists of:

A_{0}\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(B_{0},B_{0})\qquad A_{0}\longrightarrow\mathtt{c}\qquad B_{0}\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(A_{0}).

Then,
\mathtt{a}([\,],[\,])\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{0},B_{0}\,B_{0}{\Rightarrow}A_{0}\right)
and

\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\in{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{0},A_{0}{\Rightarrow}A_{0}\right)
.

This article focuses on grammars that additionally satisfy two properties called

strong connectivity
and
unambiguity
.
We first define strong connectivity.

{defi}

[Strong Connectivity
[
5
]
1
1

1

In
[
5
]
, strong connectivity is defined for
context-free specifications
. Our
definition is a straightforward adaptation of the definition for regular
tree grammars.

]
Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar.
We say that
N^{\prime}
is
reachable
from
N
if

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
is non-empty, i.e., if
there exists
a linear context
S\in\mathcal{C}({\Sigma})
such that
N\longrightarrow^{*}S[N^{\prime}]
.
We say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is
strongly connected
if for any pair
N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}
of nonterminals,

N^{\prime}
is reachable from
N
.

Remark 4
.

There is another reasonable, but slightly weaker condition of reachability:

N^{\prime}
is
reachable
from
N
if there exists
a
(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
-tree
T
such that
N\longrightarrow^{*}T
and
N^{\prime}
occurs in
T
.
The two notions coincide if
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.
Furthermore this condition can be easily fulfilled by simply removing all the nonterminals
N

with
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\emptyset
and the rules containing those nonterminals.

{exa}

The grammar
{\mathcal{G}}_{0}
in Example
2.2.2
is strongly connected,
since
\mathtt{b}([\,])\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{0},A_{0}{\Rightarrow}B_{0}\right)
and
\mathtt{a}([\,],\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{0},B_{0}{\Rightarrow}A_{0}\right)
.

Next, consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}=(\{\mathtt{a}\mapsto 2,\mathtt{b}\mapsto 1,\mathtt{c}\mapsto 0\},\{A_{1},B_{1}\},{\mathcal{R}}_{1})
, where
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
consists of:

A_{1}\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{c},A_{1})\qquad A_{1}\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(B_{1})\qquad B_{1}\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(B_{1})\qquad B_{1}\longrightarrow\mathtt{c}.

{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
is not strongly connected, since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{1},A_{1}{\Rightarrow}B_{1}\right)=\emptyset
.

To define the unambiguity of a grammar, we define (the standard notion of) leftmost rewriting.
The
leftmost rewriting relation

\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}
is the restriction of the rewriting relation that allows only the leftmost occurrence of nonterminals
to be rewritten.
Given
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
and
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
a
leftmost rewriting sequence of
T
from
N
is a sequence
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
of
(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
-trees such that

N=T_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}T_{2}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}\dots\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}T_{n}=T.

It is easy to see that
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
if and only if there exists a leftmost rewriting sequence from
N
to
T
.

{defi}
[Unambiguity]
A grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
is
unambiguous
if, for each
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
and
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
,
there exists
at most one leftmost rewriting sequence from
N
to
T
.

2.2.3.
Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem for Tree Grammars

We call a partial function
f
from
{\rm Nature}
to with infinite definitional domain
(i.e.,
\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(f)=\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid f(n)\mbox{ is defined}\}
is infinite)
a
partial sequence
, and write
f_{n}
for
f(n)
.
For a partial sequence
f=(f_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
, we define

\mathrm{def}^{f}_{(-)}
as the bijective monotonic function from
{\rm Nature}
to
\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(f)
,
and then we define:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}\triangleq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{\mathrm{def}^{f}_{n}}.

All the uses of
\limd
in this article are of the form:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{b_{n}}{b_{n}}{b_{n}}{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}}{\mathchoice{a_{n}}{a_{n}}{a_{n}}{{\scriptstyle a_{n}}}}

where
(a_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
are (non-partial) sequences (hence,
\frac{\mathchoice{b_{n}}{b_{n}}{b_{n}}{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}}{\mathchoice{a_{n}}{a_{n}}{a_{n}}{{\scriptstyle a_{n}}}}
is
undefined only when
a_{n}=0
).
The following property on
\limd

is straightforward, which we leave for the reader to check.

Lemma 5
.

If
\limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{b^{(i)}_{n}}{b^{(i)}_{n}}{b^{(i)}_{n}}{{\scriptstyle b^{(i)}_{n}}}}{\mathchoice{a^{(i)}_{n}}{a^{(i)}_{n}}{a^{(i)}_{n}}{{\scriptstyle a^{(i)}_{n}}}}=1
for
i=1,\dots,k
,
and if
0\leq b^{(i)}_{n}\leq a^{(i)}_{n}
for
i=1,\dots,k
and for
n\in{\rm Nature}
,
then
\limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{\sum_{i}b^{(i)}_{n}}{\sum_{i}b^{(i)}_{n}}{\sum_{i}b^{(i)}_{n}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{i}b^{(i)}_{n}}}}{\mathchoice{\sum_{i}a^{(i)}_{n}}{\sum_{i}a^{(i)}_{n}}{\sum_{i}a^{(i)}_{n}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{i}a^{(i)}_{n}}}}=1
.

Now we are ready to state the second main result of this article.

Theorem 6

(Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem for Regular Tree Languages)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be an unambiguous and
strongly-connected
regular tree grammar
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
,
and
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of linear contexts in
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})

such that
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exists a real constant
p>0
such that
for any
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
the following equation holds:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1.

Intuitively, the equation above says that if
n
is large enough,
almost all the trees of size
n
generated from
N
contain

S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
as a subcontext.

Remark 7
.

The standard infinite monkey theorem says that for any finite word

w=a_{1}\cdots a_{k}
over an alphabet
A
, the probability that a randomly chosen word of length
n
contains
w
as a subword converges to
1
, i.e.,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{w^{\prime}\in A^{n}\mid\exists w_{0},w_{1}\in A^{*}.w^{\prime}=w_{0}ww_{1}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}=1.

Here,
A^{*}
and
A^{n}
denote the set of all words over
A
and the set
of all words of length
n
over
A
, respectively.
This may be viewed as a special case of our parameterized infinite monkey theorem above,
where (i) the components of the grammar are given by

\Sigma=\{a\mapsto 1\mid a\in A\}\cup\{e{\,\mapsto 0}\}
,
{\mathcal{N}}=\{N\}
, and

{\mathcal{R}}=\{N\longrightarrow a(N)\mid a\in A\}\cup\{N\longrightarrow e\}
, and (ii)

S_{n}=a_{1}(\cdots a_{k}([\,])\cdots)
independently of
n
.

Note that in the theorem above, we have used
\limd
rather than
\lim
.
To avoid the use of
\limd
, we need an additional condition on
{\mathcal{G}}
, called
aperiodicity.

{defi}
[Aperiodicity
[
5
]
]
Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a grammar.
For a nonterminal
N
,
{\mathcal{G}}
is called
N
-
aperiodic
if
there exists
n_{0}
such that

\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left(N\right)\right)>0
for any
n\geq n_{0}
.
Further,
{\mathcal{G}}
is called
aperiodic
if

{\mathcal{G}}
is
N
-aperiodic for any nonterminal
N
.

In Theorem
6
,
if we further assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is
N
-aperiodic,
then
\limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}
in the statement can be replaced with

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}
.

In the rest of this section, we reformulate the theorem above in the form more convenient for
proving Theorem
2
.
In Definition
2.1
,

\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)
was defined
as the disjoint union of
(\Lambda_{n}(\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi))_{\tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota)}
.
To prove Theorem
2
, we will construct a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
so that, for each
\tau
,

\Lambda_{n}(\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\cong{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
holds for some nonterminal
N
.
Thus, the following style of statement is more convenient, which is obtained as an immediate corollary of
Theorem
6

and Lemma
5
.

Corollary 8
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be an unambiguous and
strongly-connected regular tree grammar such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
,
and
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of linear contexts in
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})

such that
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exists a real constant
p>0
such that
for any non-empty set
\mathcal{I}
of nonterminals of
{\mathcal{G}}
the following equation holds:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{(N,T)\in\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1.

We can also replace
a family of linear contexts
(S_{n})_{n}
above
with that of trees
(T_{n})_{n}
. We need only this corollary in the proof of Theorem
2
.

Corollary 9
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be an unambiguous
and strongly-connected regular tree grammar
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
and
there exists
C\in\cup_{\kappa}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
with
\mathtt{hn}(C)\geq 2
.
Also, let
(T_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of trees in

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)

such that
|T_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exists a real constant
p>0
such that
for any non-empty set
\mathcal{I}
of nonterminals of
{\mathcal{G}}
the following equation holds:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{(N,T)\in\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid T_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1.

Proof 2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
.
Let
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
be the context in the assumption and let
\kappa
be of the form
N^{\prime}_{1}\cdots N^{\prime}_{k}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
, i.e.,
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}C[N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k}]
, where
k=\mathtt{hn}(C)\geq 2
.
By Corollary
8
, it suffices to construct a family of
linear contexts
(S_{n})_{n}
such that (i)
S_{n}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})
,
(ii)
T_{n}\preceq S_{n}
, and (iii)
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
For each
n
, there exists
N_{n}
such that

N_{n}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{n}
.
Since
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
and by the strong connectivity,
for each
i\leq k
there exists
T^{\prime}_{i}
such that

N^{\prime}_{i}\longrightarrow^{*}T^{\prime}_{i}
.
For each
n
, by the strong connectivity, there exists

S^{\prime}_{n}
such that
N_{1}^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}S^{\prime}_{n}[N_{n}]
,
and since
{\mathcal{N}}
is finite, we can choose
S^{\prime}_{n}
so that the size is bounded above by a constant (that is independent of
n
). Let
S_{n}
be
C[S^{\prime}_{n}[T_{n}],[\,],T^{\prime}_{3},\dots,T^{\prime}_{k}]
.
Then (i)
S_{n}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}_{2}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})
because
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}C[N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k}]\longrightarrow^{*}C[S^{\prime}_{n}[T_{n}],N^{\prime}_{2},T^{\prime}_{3},\dots,T^{\prime}_{k}]
,
(ii)
T_{n}\preceq S_{n}
, and (iii)
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
, as required.

Remark 10
.

In each of Corollaries
8
and
9
,
if we further assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is
N
-aperiodic for any
N\in\mathcal{I}
,
then
\limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}
in the statement can be replaced with

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}
.

Remark 11
.

In Theorem
2
(and similarly in Corollaries
8
and
9
),
one might be interested in the following form of probability:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\uplus_{m\leq n}\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{m}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log m\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\uplus_{m\leq n}{\mathcal{L}}_{m}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1,

which discusses trees of size
at most

n
rather than
exactly

n
.
Under the assumption of aperiodicity,
the above equation follows from Theorem
2
,
by the following
Stolz-Cesàro theorem

[
23
, Theorem 1.22]
:
Let
(a_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a sequence of real numbers and
assume
b_{n}>0
and
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}=\infty
. Then
for any
c\in[-\infty,+\infty]
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}=c\qquad\text{implies}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{\sum_{m\leq n}a_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}a_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}a_{m}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{m\leq n}a_{m}}}}{\mathchoice{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}}}=c.

(We also remark that the inverse implication also holds if there exists
\gamma\in\real\setminus\{1\}
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{m\leq n}b_{m}}}}{\mathchoice{\sum_{m\leq n+1}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n+1}b_{m}}{\sum_{m\leq n+1}b_{m}}{{\scriptstyle\sum_{m\leq n+1}b_{m}}}}=\gamma

[
23
, Theorem 1.23]
.)

3.
Proof of the Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem for Regular Tree Languages (Theorem
6
)

Here we prove Theorem
6
.
In Section
3.1
, we first prove
a parameterized version
2
2

2

Although the parameterization is a simple extension, we are not aware of
literature that explicitly states this parameterized version.
of infinite monkey
theorem for
words
, and explain how the proof for the word case can be extended to deal with
regular tree languages. The structure of the rest of the section is explained at the end of Section
3.1
.

3.1.
Proof for Word Case and Outline of this Section

Let
A
be an alphabet, i.e., a finite non-empty set of symbols.
For a word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
over
A
,
We write
|w|
for
n
and call it the
size
(or length) of
w
.
As usual, we denote by
A^{n}
the set of all words of size
n
over
A
,
and by
A^{*}
the set of all finite words over
A
:
A^{*}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}A^{n}
.
For two words
w,w^{\prime}\in A^{*}
,
we say
w^{\prime}
is a
subword of
w
and write
w^{\prime}\sqsubseteq w
if
w=w_{1}w^{\prime}w_{2}
for
some words
w_{1},w_{2}\in A^{*}
.
The infinite monkey theorem
states that, for any word
w\in A^{*}
,
the probability that a randomly chosen word of size
n
contains
w
as
a subword tends to one if
n
tends to infinity (recall Remark
7
).
The following theorem is
a parameterized version, where
w
may depend on
n
.
It may also be viewed as a special case of Theorem
6
, where

\Sigma=\{a\mapsto 1\mid a\in A\}\cup\{e\}
,
{\mathcal{N}}=\{N\}
,

{\mathcal{R}}=\{N\longrightarrow a(N)\mid a\in A\}\cup\{N\longrightarrow e\}
, and

S_{n}=a_{1}(\cdots a_{k}([\,])\cdots)
for each
w_{n}=a_{1}\cdots a_{k}
.

Proposition 12

(Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem for Words)
.

Let
A
be an alphabet and
(w_{n})_{n}
be a family of words over
A
such
that
|w_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then, there exists a real constant
p>0
such that we have:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(\{w\in A^{n}\mid w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\sqsubseteq w\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\{w\in A^{n}\mid w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\sqsubseteq w\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\{w\in A^{n}\mid w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\sqsubseteq w\}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(\{w\in A^{n}\mid w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\sqsubseteq w\}\right)}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}}}=1.

Proof 3.1
.

Let
Z_{n}
be
1-\#\!\left(\{w\in A^{n}\mid w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\sqsubseteq w\}\right)/\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)
,
i.e., the probability that a word of size
n
does
not
contain
w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
.
By the assumption
|w_{n}|=O(n)
,
there exists
b>0
such that
|w_{n}|\leq bn
for sufficiently large
n
.
Let
0<q<1
be an arbitrary real number, and we define

p\triangleq\frac{q}{2b\log{\#\!\left(A\right)}}
.
We write
s(n)
for
|w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}|

and
c(n)
for

\lfloor n/s(n)\rfloor
.
Let
n\in{\rm Nature}
.
Given a word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}\in A^{n}
, let us decompose it to subwords of length
s(n)
as follows.

w=\underbrace{a_{1}\cdots a_{s(n)}}_{\text{first subword}}\cdots\underbrace{a_{(c(n)-1)s(n)+1}\cdots a_{c(n)s(n)}}_{c(n)\text{-th subword}}\underbrace{a_{c(n)s(n)+1}\cdots a_{n}}_{\text{remainder}}.

Then,

\displaystyle 0\leq Z_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{``the probability that none of the $i$-th subword is $w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}$''}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathchoice{\#\!\left((A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\})^{c(n)}A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left((A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\})^{c(n)}A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left((A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\})^{c(n)}A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left((A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\})^{c(n)}A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\right)}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{n}\right)}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\setminus\{w_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\}\right)}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}}}\right)^{c(n)}=\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)-1}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)-1}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)-1}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)-1}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)}}}\right)^{c(n)}=\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}}}\right)^{c(n)}

and we can show
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}}}\right)^{c(n)}=0

as follows.

For sufficiently large
n
,
we have
s(n)\leq b\lceil p\log n\rceil\leq 2bp\log n=q\frac{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}{\mathchoice{\log{\#\!\left(A\right)}}{\log{\#\!\left(A\right)}}{\log{\#\!\left(A\right)}}{{\scriptstyle\log{\#\!\left(A\right)}}}}=q\log_{\#\!\left(A\right)}n
,
and hence

\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}{{\scriptstyle\#\!\left(A\right)^{s(n)}}}}\right)^{c(n)}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{c(n)}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{s(n)}{s(n)}{s(n)}{{\scriptstyle s(n)}}}-1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{\log\#\!\left(A\right)}{\log\#\!\left(A\right)}{\log\#\!\left(A\right)}{{\scriptstyle\log\#\!\left(A\right)}}}{\mathchoice{q}{q}{q}{{\scriptstyle q}}}\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}-1}.

Also we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}}=0
(which we leave for the reader to check);
therefore
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Z_{n}=0
.

The key observations in the above proof were:

(W1)

Each word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
can be decomposed to

(w_{\mathtt{rem}}^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{c(n)})\in A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\times\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{c(n)}A^{s(n)}\Big)

where
w_{\mathtt{rem}}^{\prime}=a_{c(n)s(n)+1}\cdots a_{n}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}=a_{(i-1)s(n)+1}\cdots a_{is(n)}
.

(W2)

The word decomposition above induces the following
decomposition of the set
A^{n}
of words of length
n
:

\displaystyle A^{n}\

\displaystyle\cong\ A^{n-c(n)s(n)}\times\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{c(n)}A^{s(n)}\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\ \coprod_{w\in A^{n-c(n)s(n)}}\prod_{i=1}^{c(n)}A^{s(n)}

Here,
\cong
denotes the existence of a bijection.

(W3)

Choose
s(n)
and
c(n)
so that
(i)
A^{s(n)}
contains at least one element that contains
w_{n}
as a subword
and (ii)
c(n)
is sufficiently large. By condition (i), the probability that
an element of
A^{n}
does not contain
w
as a subword can be bounded above
the probability that none of
w^{\prime}_{i}\,(i\in\{1,\ldots,c(n)\})
contains
w_{n}
as a subword,
i.e.,
(1-\frac{1}{\#\!\left(A^{s(n)}\right)})^{c(n)}
, which converges to
0
by condition (ii).

The proof of Theorem
6
in the rest of this section is based on similar observations:

(T1)

Each tree
T
of size
n
can be decomposed to

(E,U_{1},\ldots,U_{c_{E}}),

where
U_{1},\ldots,U_{c_{E}}
are (affine) subcontexts,

E
, called a
second-order context
(which will be formally defined later),
is the “remainder” of
T
obtained by extracting
U_{1},\ldots,U_{c_{E}}
, and

c_{E}
is a number that depends on
E
.
For example, the tree on the lefthand side of Figure
4

can be decomposed to the second-order context and affine contexts
shown on the righthand side. By substituting each affine context for

\llbracket\,\rrbracket
in the preorder traversal order, we recover the tree on the lefthand side.
This decomposition of a tree may be regarded as a generalization of the word decomposition
above (by viewing a word as an affine context), where the part
E

corresponds to the remainder

a_{c(n)s(n)+1}\cdots a_{n}
of the word decomposition.

(T2)

The tree decomposition above induces
the decomposition of
the set
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
in the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\prod_{j=1}^{c_{E}}\mathcal{U}_{E,j}

(1)

where

\mathcal{E}
is a set of second-order contexts and

\mathcal{U}_{E,j}
is a set of affine contexts.
(At this point, the reader need not be concerned about
the exact definitions of
\mathcal{E}
and

\mathcal{U}_{E,j}
, which will be given later.)

(T3)

Design the above decomposition so that
(i) each
\mathcal{U}_{E,j}
contains at least one element that
contains
S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
as a subcontext,
and (ii)
c_{E}
is sufficiently large.
By condition (i),
the probability that an element of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
does not contain
S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}

as a subcontext is bounded above by
the probability that none of
U_{i}\,(i\in\{1,\ldots,c_{E}\})

contain
S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
as a subcontext,
which is further bounded above by

\displaystyle{\max_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{c_{E}}(1-\frac{1}{\#\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{E,j}\right)})\Big)}
.
The bound can be proved to converge to
0
by using condition (ii).

Figure 4
.
An example of tree decomposition. The parts
surrounded by rectangles
on the lefthand side show the extracted affine contexts, and the remaining part of
the tree is
the second-order tree context.

The rest of the section is organized as follows.
Before we jump to the decomposition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, we
first present
a decomposition of
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
, i.e., the set of
\Sigma
-trees of size
n

in Section
3.2
. The decomposition of

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
may be a special case of that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, where

{\mathcal{G}}
generates all the trees in
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
.
For a technical convenience in extending the decomposition of

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
to that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
,
we normalize grammars to
canonical form

in Section
3.3
. We then give the decomposition of

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
given in Equation (
1
) above,
and prove that it satisfies the required properties
in Sections
3.4
and

3.5
.
Finally, we prove Theorem
6
in Section
3.6
.

3.2.
Grammar-Independent Tree Decomposition

In this subsection, we will define a decomposition
function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
(where
m
is a parameter) that decomposes a tree
T

into (i) a (sufficiently long) sequence
P=U_{1}\cdots U_{k}

consisting of affine subcontexts of size no less than
m
,
and (ii) a “second-order” context
E
(defined
shortly in Section
3.2.1
), which is the remainder of extracting
P
from
T
.
Recall Figure
4
, which illustrates how a tree is decomposed by

\Phi_{3}^{\bullet}
. Here, the symbol
\llbracket\,\rrbracket
in the second-order context on the
right-hand side represents the original position of each subcontext.
By filling the
i
-th occurrence (counted in the depth-first, left-to-right pre-order)
of
\llbracket\,\rrbracket
with
the
i
-th affine context, we can recover the original tree on the left hand side.

As formally stated later (in Corollary
16
),
the decomposition function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
provides a witness for a bijection of the form

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\prod_{j=1}^{c_{E}}\mathcal{U}_{E,j},

a special case of Equation (
1
) mentioned in Section
3.1
.

3.2.1.
Second-Order Contexts

We first define the notion of second-order contexts and operations on them.

The set of
second-order contexts
over
\Sigma
,
ranged over by
E
, is defined by:

E::=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]\;\mid\;a(E_{1},\ldots,E_{\Sigma(a)})\ \ (a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Sigma)).

Intuitively, the second-order context is an expression having holes of
the form
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}
(called
second-order holes
), which should be filled with a
k
-context of size
n
.
By filling all the second-order holes, we obtain a
\Sigma
-tree.
Note that

k
may be
0
. In the technical development below,
we only consider second-order holes
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}
such that

k
is
0
or
1
.
We write
\mathtt{shn}(E)
for the number of the second-order holes in
E
.
Note that
\Sigma
-trees can be regarded as second-order contexts
E
such that
\mathtt{shn}(E)=0
, and vice versa.
For
i\leq\mathtt{shn}(E)
, we write
E{\centerdot}i
for the
i
-th second-order hole
(counted in the depth-first, left-to-right pre-order).
We define the
size

|E|
by:

|\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{k}[E_{1},\dots,E_{k}]|\triangleq n+|E_{1}|+\cdots+|E_{k}|

and

|a(E_{1},\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)})|\triangleq|E_{1}|+\cdots+|E_{\Sigma(a)}|+1
.
Note that
|E|
includes the size of contexts to fill the second-order holes in
E
.

{exa}

The second-order context on the right hand side of Figure
4

is expressed as
E=\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))])
, where
\mathtt{shn}(E)=3
,

|E|=14
,
E{\centerdot}1=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}
,
E{\centerdot}2=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3}
, and
E{\centerdot}3=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}
.

Next we define the substitution operation on second-order contexts.
For a context
C
and a second-order hole
\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{k}
,
we write
C\mathbin{:}\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{k}
if
C
is a
k
-context of size
n
.
Given
E
and
C
such that
\mathtt{shn}(E)\geq 1
and

C\mathbin{:}E{\centerdot}1
, we write
E\llbracket C\rrbracket
for the second-order context obtained by replacing the leftmost second-order hole of
E
(i.e.,
E{\centerdot}1
)
with
C
(and by interpreting the syntactical bracket
[-]
as the substitution operation).
Formally, it is defined by induction on
E
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\left(\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{k}[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]\right)\llbracket C\rrbracket&\triangleq&C[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]\\
(a(E_{1},\ldots,E_{\Sigma(a)}))\llbracket C\rrbracket&\triangleq&a(E_{1},\ldots,E_{i}\llbracket C\rrbracket,\ldots,E_{\Sigma(a)})\\
&&\hfill\text{ where }i=\min\{j\mid\mathtt{shn}(E_{j})\geq 1,1\leq j\leq\Sigma(a)\}.\end{array}

In the first clause,
C[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]
is the second-order context obtained by
replacing
[\,]_{i}
in
C
with
E_{i}
for each
i\leq k
.
Note that
we have
|E\llbracket C\rrbracket|=|E|
whenever
E\llbracket C\rrbracket
is well-defined,
i.e., if
C\mathbin{:}E{\centerdot}1
(cf. Lemma
13
below).

We extend the substitution operation for a sequence of contexts.
We use metavariable
P
for sequences of contexts.
For
E
and
P=C_{1}C_{2}\cdots C_{\mathtt{shn}(E)}
, we
write
P\mathbin{:}E
if
C_{i}\mathbin{:}E{\centerdot}i
for each
i\leq\mathtt{shn}(E)
.
Given
E
and a sequence of contexts
P=C_{1}C_{2}\cdots C_{\ell}
such that

\ell\leq\mathtt{shn}(E)
and
C_{i}\mathbin{:}E{\centerdot}i
for each
i\leq\ell
,
we define
E\llbracket P\rrbracket
by induction on
P
:

\displaystyle E\llbracket\epsilon\rrbracket\triangleq E

\displaystyle E\llbracket C\cdot P\rrbracket\triangleq(E\llbracket C\rrbracket)\llbracket P\rrbracket

Note that
\mathtt{shn}(E\llbracket C\rrbracket)=\mathtt{shn}(E)-1
, so if
P\mathbin{:}E

then
E\llbracket P\rrbracket
is a tree.

{exa}

Recall the second-order context
E=\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))])
in
Figure
4
and Example
3.2.1
.
Let
P
be the sequence of affine contexts given in Figure
4
:

\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c})\cdot\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})).

Then

E\llbracket P{\centerdot}1\rrbracket=\mathtt{b}(P{\centerdot}1[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))])=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))),\mathtt{c})),

and

\displaystyle E\llbracket P\rrbracket

\displaystyle=((E\llbracket P{\centerdot}1\rrbracket])\llbracket P{\centerdot}2\rrbracket)\llbracket P{\centerdot}3\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(P{\centerdot}2,\mathtt{b}(P{\centerdot}3))),\mathtt{c}))=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))))),\mathtt{c})),

which is the tree shown on
the left hand side of Figure
4
.

Thanks to the size annotation, the substitution operation preserves the size of a second-order context.

Lemma 13
.

Let
E
be a second-order context and
P
be a sequence of contexts such that
P\mathbin{:}E
.
Then
|E|=|E\llbracket P\rrbracket|
.

Proof 3.2
.

The proof is given by a straightforward induction on
E
. In the case
E=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]
,
we use the fact
|C[E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}]|=|C|+|E_{1}|+\cdots+|E_{k}|
(which can be shown by induction on
C
).

3.2.2.
Grammar-Independent Decomposition Function

Now we define the decomposition function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
.
Let
\Sigma
be a ranked alphabet and
m\geq 1
.
We define
r_{\Sigma}\triangleq\max(\mathrm{Im}\left(\Sigma\right))
, which is also written as
r
for short.
We always assume that
r\geq 1
,
since it holds whenever
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
,
which is an assumption of Theorem
6
.
We shall define the decomposition function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet,\Sigma}

(we omit
\Sigma
and just write
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
for
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet,\Sigma}
below)
so that
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)=(E,P)
where
(i)
E
is the second-order context, and (ii)

P
is a sequence of affine contexts, (iii)
E\llbracket P\rrbracket=T
,
and (iv)
m\leq|P{\centerdot}i|\leq r(m-1)+1
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,\#\!\left(P\right)\}
.

The function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
is defined as follows, using an auxiliary decomposition function

\Phi_{m}
given below.

\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)\triangleq(U[E],P)\mbox{ where }(U,E,P)=\Phi_{m}(T).

The auxiliary decomposition function (just called “decomposition function” below)
\Phi_{m}

traverses a given tree
T
in a bottom-up manner,
extracts a sequence
P
of subcontexts,
and returns it along with a linear context
U
and a second-order context
E
;

U
and
E
together represent
the “remainder” of extracting
P
from
T
.
During the bottom-up traversal, the
U
-component for each subtree
T^{\prime}
represents a context
containing the root of
T^{\prime}
;
whether it is extracted as (a part of) a subcontext or becomes a part of the remainder will be decided later based on the surrounding context.
The
E
-component will stay as a part of the remainder during
the decomposition of the whole tree (unless the current subtree
T^{\prime}
is too small, i.e.,
|T^{\prime}|<m
).

We define
\Phi_{m}
by:

•

If
|T|<m
, then
\Phi_{m}(T)\triangleq([\,],T,\epsilon)
.

•

If
|T|\geq m
,
T=a(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
, and

\Phi_{m}(T_{i})=(U_{i},E_{i},P_{i})\ (\mbox{for each }i\leq\Sigma(a))
, then:

\Phi_{m}(T)\triangleq\left\{\begin{aligned} &([\,],\;a\bigl(U_{1}[E_{1}],\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)}[E_{\Sigma(a)}]\bigr),\;P_{1}\cdots P_{\Sigma(a)})\\
&\quad\mbox{if there exist $i,j$ such that $1\leq i<j\leq\Sigma(a)$
and $|T_{i}|,|T_{j}|\geq m$}\\
&([\,],\;\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{1}[E_{i}],\;a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})\cdot P_{i})\\
&\quad\mbox{if $|T_{j}|<m$ for every $j\neq i$, $|T_{i}|\geq m$, and}\\
&\quad\mbox{\phantom{if} $n\triangleq|a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})|\geq m$}\\
&(a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)}),\;E_{i},\;P_{i})\\
&\quad\mbox{if $|T_{j}|<m$ for every $j\neq i$, $|T_{i}|\geq m$, and}\\
&\quad\mbox{\phantom{if} $|a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})|<m$}\\
&([\,],\;\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{0},\;T)\\
&\quad\mbox{if $|T_{i}|<m$ for every $i\leq\Sigma(a)$, and $n\triangleq|T|$ }\end{aligned}\right.

(2)

As defined above, the decomposition is carried out by case analysis on the size of a given tree.
If
T
is not large enough (i.e.,
|T|<m
), then
\Phi_{m}(T)
returns
an empty sequence of contexts, while keeping
T
in the
E
-component.
If
|T|\geq m
, then
\Phi_{m}(T)
returns a non-empty sequence of contexts, by case analysis on the
sizes of
T
’s subtrees. If there are more than one subtree whose size is no less than
m
(the first case above),
then
\Phi_{m}(T)
concatenates the sequences of contexts extracted from the subtrees, and returns the remainder
as the second-order context. If only one of the subtrees, say
T_{i}
, is large enough (the second and third cases), then
it basically returns the sequence
P_{i}
extracted from
T_{i}
; however, if the remaining part

a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
is also large enough, then it is added to the sequence (the second case).
If none of the subtrees is large enough (but
T
is large enough), then
T
is returned as the
P
-component
(the last case).

{exa}

Recall Figure
4
. Let
T_{0}
be the tree on the left hand side.
For some of the subtrees of
T_{0}
,

\Phi_{3}
can be calculated as follows.

\begin{array}[]{l}\Phi_{3}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))=([\,],\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\epsilon)\\
\Phi_{3}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))=([\,],\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))\hfill\mbox{ (by the last case of~\eqref{eq:defOfDecomp})}\\
\Phi_{3}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))=([\,],\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5},\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))\qquad\hfill\mbox{ (by the last case of~\eqref{eq:defOfDecomp})}\\
\Phi_{3}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})),\mathtt{b}(\cdots)))=([\,],\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5})),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))\\
\hfill\mbox{ (by the first case of~\eqref{eq:defOfDecomp})}\\
\Phi_{3}(T_{0})=(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))],\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c})\cdot\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))\\
\hfill\mbox{ (by the third case of~\eqref{eq:defOfDecomp})}\\
\end{array}

From
\Phi_{3}(T_{0})
above, we obtain:

\Phi_{3}^{\bullet}(T_{0})=\left(\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))]),\ \mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c})\cdot\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\right).

3.2.3.
Properties of the Decomposition Function

We summarize important properties of
\Phi_{m}
in this subsection.

We say that an affine context
U
is
good for
m

if

|U|\geq m
and

U
is of the form
a(U_{1},\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)})

where
|U_{i}|<m
for each
i\leq\Sigma(a)
.
In other words,
U
is good if
U
is of an appropriate size:
it is large enough (i.e.
|U|\geq m
), and not too large (i.e. the size
of any proper subterm is less than
m
).
For example,
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))
is good for
3
, but
neither
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}([\,]))
nor
\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})))
is.

The following are basic properties of the auxiliary decomposition function

\Phi_{m}
.
The property (
1
) says that the original tree can be
recovered by composing the elements obtained by the decomposition,
and the property
(
3
)
ensures that
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
extracts only good contexts from
T
.

Lemma 14
.

Let T be a tree. If
\Phi_{m}(T)=(U,E,P)
, then:

(1)

P\mathbin{:}E
,
\mathtt{hn}(U)=1
, and
(U[E])\llbracket P\rrbracket=T
.

(2)

|U|<m
.

(3)

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,\#\!\left(P\right)\}
,

P{\centerdot}i
is good for
m
.

(4)

|T|=|U[E]|
.

Proof 3.3
.

(
1
)–(
3
) follow by straightforward simultaneous induction on
|T|
.
(
4
) follows from (
1
) and
Lemma
13
.

The following lemma ensures that
\mathtt{shn}(E)
is sufficiently large whenever
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)=(E,P)
.
Recall condition (ii) of (T3) in Section
3.1
;
\mathtt{shn}(E)
corresponds to
c_{E}
.

Lemma 15
.

For any tree
T
and
m
such that
1\leq m\leq|T|
,
if

\Phi_{m}(T)=(U,E,P)
, then

\#\!\left(P\right)\geq\frac{|T|}{2rm}.

Proof 3.4
.

Recall that
r\triangleq\max(\mathrm{Im}\left(\Sigma\right))
and we assume
r\geq 1
.
We show below that

\displaystyle|U|+2rm\#\!\left(P\right)-rm\geq|T|

by induction on
T
.
Then it follows that

2rm\#\!\left(P\right)\geq|T|+rm-|U|>|T|+rm-m\geq|T|

(where the second inequality uses Lemma
14
(
2
)),
which implies
\#\!\left(P\right)>\frac{\mathchoice{|T|}{|T|}{|T|}{{\scriptstyle|T|}}}{\mathchoice{2rm}{2rm}{2rm}{{\scriptstyle 2rm}}}
as required.

Since
|T|\geq m
,
\Phi_{m}(T)
is computed by Equation (
2
),
on which we perform a case analysis.
Let
T=a(T_{1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
and
\Phi_{m}(T_{i})=(U_{i},E_{i},P_{i})\ (i\leq\Sigma(a))
.

•

The first case:
In this case, we have

U=[\,]\qquad P=P_{i_{1}}P_{i_{2}}\cdots P_{i_{s}}

where
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\}=\{i\leq\Sigma(a)\mid|T_{i}|\geq m\}
and
s\geq 2
,
since if
|T_{i}|<m
then
P_{i}=\epsilon
.
Note that we have
r\geq 2
in this case.
Then,

\displaystyle|U|+2rm\#\!\left(P\right)-rm

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 2rm\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}\#\!\left(P_{i_{j}}\right)\right)-rm

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}(|T_{i_{j}}|+rm-|U_{i_{j}}|)\right)-rm\qquad(\because\text{by induction hypothesis for $T_{i_{j}}$})

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}(|T_{i_{j}}|+rm-(m-1))\right)-rm\qquad(\because|U_{i_{j}}|\leq m-1\mbox{ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:charphi}(\ref{charphi:garbagesize})})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}|T_{i_{j}}|\right)+srm-sm+s-rm

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}|T_{i_{j}}|\right)+2rm-sm+s-rm-r+1\qquad(\because\text{$s\geq 2$ and $r\geq 1$})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}|T_{i_{j}}|\right)+(r-s)(m-1)+1

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}|T_{i_{j}}|\right)+(\Sigma(a)-s)(m-1)+1\qquad(\because r\geq\Sigma(a))

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\sum_{j\leq s}|T_{i_{j}}|\right)+(\textstyle\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,\Sigma(a)\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}}|T_{i}|)+1

\displaystyle\qquad(\because m-1\geq|T_{i}|\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,\Sigma(a)\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle|T|

as required.

•

The second case:
In this case, we have

U=[\,]\qquad P=a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})P_{i}

and
|T_{j}|\geq m
if and only if
j=i
.
Also we have
r\geq 1
.
Then,

\displaystyle|U|+2rm\#\!\left(P\right)-rm

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 2rm(1+\#\!\left(P_{i}\right))-rm

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle rm+2rm\#\!\left(P_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle rm+(|T_{i}|+rm-|U_{i}|)\qquad(\because\text{by induction hypothesis for $T_{i}$})

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle|T_{i}|+rm-(m-1)\qquad(\because rm\geq 0,|U_{i}|\leq m-1\mbox{ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:charphi}(\ref{charphi:garbagesize})})

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle|T_{i}|+rm-r-m+2\qquad(\because r\geq 1)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle|T_{i}|+(r-1)(m-1)+1

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle|T_{i}|+(\Sigma(a)-1)(m-1)+1\qquad(\because r\geq\Sigma(a))

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle|T_{i}|+(\textstyle\sum_{j\in\{1,\ldots,\Sigma(a)\}\setminus\{i\}}|T_{j}|)+1

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle|T|

as required.

•

The third case of (
2
):
In this case, we have

U=a(T_{1},\ldots,U_{i},\ldots,T_{\Sigma(a)})\qquad P=P_{i}

and
|T_{j}|\geq m
if and only if
j=i
.
Then,

\displaystyle|U|+2rm\#\!\left(P\right)-rm

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 1+(\textstyle\sum_{j\neq i}|T_{j}|)+|U_{i}|+2rm\#\!\left(P_{i}\right)-rm

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle 1+(\textstyle\sum_{j\neq i}|T_{j}|)+|T_{i}|\qquad(\because\text{by induction hypothesis for $T_{i}$})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle|T|

as required.

•

The fourth case of (
2
):
In this case, we have

U=[\,]\qquad P=T.

Then

\displaystyle|U|+2rm\#\!\left(P\right)-rm

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle rm

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle r(m-1)+1\qquad(\because r\geq 1)

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\Sigma(a)(m-1)+1\qquad(\because r\geq\Sigma(a))

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle(\textstyle\sum_{j\in\{1,\ldots,\Sigma(a)\}}|T_{j}|)+1

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle|T|

as required.

3.2.4.
Decomposition of
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)

This subsection shows that
the decomposition function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
above provides a witness for a bijection of the form

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\prod_{j=1}^{c_{E}}\mathcal{U}_{E,\,j}.

We prepare some definitions to precisely state the bijection.
We define the set
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
of second-order contexts and the set
\mathcal{U}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}}
of affine contexts
by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{E\mid(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)\text{ for some }T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\text{ and }P\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{U\mid U:\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n},U\text{ is good for }m\}.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
is the set of second-order contexts obtained by decomposing a tree of
size
n
, and
\mathcal{U}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}}
is the set of good contexts that match the second-order context

\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}
.

The bijection is then stated as the following lemma.

Lemma 16
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\;\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}.

(3)

The rest of this subsection is devoted to a proof of the lemma above;
readers may wish to skip the rest of this subsection upon the first reading.

Lemma 17
.

If
E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
, then
|E|=n
.

Proof 3.5
.

Suppose
E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
. Then

(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
for some
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
and
P
.
By Lemma

14
(
4
),

|E|=|T|=n
.

For a second-order context
E
,
and
m\geq 1
,
we define the set
\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
of sequences of affine contexts:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{P\mid(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)\text{ for some }T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)\}.

The set
\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
consists of sequences
P
of affine contexts that
match
E
and are obtained by the decomposition function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
.
In the rest of this subsection,
we prove the bijection in
Lemma
16

in two steps.
We first show
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
(Lemma
18
,
called “coproduct lemma”), and then show

\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}=\prod_{j=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}j}
(Lemma
18
, called “product lemma”).

Lemma 18

(Coproduct Lemma (for Grammar-Independent Decomposition))
.

For any

n\geq 1
and
m\geq 1
,
there exists a bijection

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}

that maps each element
(E,P)
of the set

\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
to
E\llbracket P\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

Proof 3.6
.

We define a function

f:\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)

by
f(E,P)\triangleq E\llbracket P\rrbracket
,
and a function

g:{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}

by
g(T)\triangleq\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
.

Let us check that these are functions into the codomains:

•

f(E,P)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
:
Since
P\in\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
, there exists
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
such that

(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
.
By Lemma
14
(
1
),
we have
f(E,P)=E\llbracket P\rrbracket=T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
.
By the condition
E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
and by
Lemmas
13
and
17
,

|E\llbracket P\rrbracket|=|E|=n
. Thus,
f(E,P)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
as required.

•

g(T)\in\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
:
Obvious from the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}
and
\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
.

We have
f(g(T))=T
by Lemmas
14
(
1
).
Let
(E,P)\in\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
.
By definition, there exists
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
such that
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
.
By using Lemmas
13
and
17
again,
we have
|T|=|E\llbracket P\rrbracket|=|E|=n
. Thus,
(E,P)=g(T)
.
Then

g(f(E,P))=g(f(g(T))=g(T)=(E,P).

It remains to show the product lemma:

\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}=\prod_{j=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}j}
.
To this end, we prove a few more properties about the auxiliary decomposition function
\Phi_{m}
.

Lemma 19
.

If
|T|\geq m
and
\Phi_{m}(T)=(U,E,P)
, then
|E|\geq m
.

Proof 3.7
.

Straightforward induction on
T
.

The following lemma states that, given
\Phi_{m}(U[T])=(U,E,P)
,
P
is
determined only by
T
(
U
does not matter); this is because the decomposition is
performed in a bottom-up manner.

Lemma 20
.

For
m\geq 1
,
E
,
P
,
T
, and a linear context
U
with
|T|\geq m
and
|U|<m
,
we have
\Phi_{m}(T)=([\,],E,P)
if and only if
\Phi_{m}(U[T])=(U,E,P)
.

Proof 3.8
.

The proof proceeds by induction on
|U|
.
If
U=[\,]
the claim trivially holds.
If
U\neq[\,]
,
U
is of the form
a(T_{1},\dots,U_{i},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
.
Since
|U|<m
, we have
|U_{i}|<m
and
|T_{j}|<m
for every
j\neq i
.

Assume that
\Phi_{m}(T)=([\,],E,P)
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have
\Phi_{m}(U_{i}[T])=(U_{i},E,P)
.
Since
|U|=|a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},U_{i},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})|<m
, we should apply the third case of Equation (
2
) to compute
\Phi_{m}(U[T])
.
Hence, we have
