\Phi_{m}(U[T])=(a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},U_{i},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)}),E,P)=(U,E,P).

Conversely, assume that

\Phi_{m}(U[T])=(U,E,P)=(a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},U_{i},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)}),E,P).

Let
(U^{\prime}_{i},E^{\prime}_{i},P^{\prime}_{i})=\Phi_{m}(U_{i}[T])
and
(U^{\prime}_{j},E^{\prime}_{j},P^{\prime}_{j})=\Phi_{m}(T_{j})
for each
j\neq i
.
Then the final step in the computation of
\Phi_{m}(U[T])
must be the third case; otherwise
U=[\,]
, a contradiction.
By the position of the unique hole in
U
, it must be the case that
U=a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},U^{\prime}_{i},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
,
E=E^{\prime}_{i}
and
P=P^{\prime}_{i}
.
So
(U_{i},E,P)=\Phi_{m}(U_{i}[T])
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\Phi_{m}(T)=([\,],E,P)
.

The following is the key lemma for the product lemma, which says that
if
(U,E,P)=\Phi_{m}(T)
, the decomposition is actually independent of the
P
-part.

Lemma 21
.

If
(U,E,P)=\Phi_{m}(T)
, then

\Phi_{m}(U[E]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(U,E,P^{\prime})
for any
P^{\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}
.

Proof 3.9
.

The proof proceeds by induction on
|T|(=|U[E]|)
. If
|T|<m
, then
E=T
, hence
\mathtt{shn}(E)=0
. Thus,

P=P^{\prime}=\epsilon
, which implies

\Phi_{m}(U[E]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=\Phi_{m}(T)=(U,E,P)=(U,E,P^{\prime})
.

For the case
|T|\geq m
,
we proceed by the case analysis on which rule of
Equation (
2
) was
used to compute
(U,E,P)=\Phi_{m}(T)
.
Assume that
T=a(T_{1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
and
(U_{i},E_{i},P_{i})=\Phi_{m}(T_{i})
for each
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
By Lemma
14
, we have
P_{i}\mathbin{:}E_{i}
for each
i
.

•

The first case of Equation (
2
):
We have
|T_{j}|,|T_{j^{\prime}}|\geq m

for some
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq\Sigma(a)
, and:

U=[\,]\qquad E=a(U_{1}[E_{1}],\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)}[E_{\Sigma(a)}])\qquad P=P_{1}\cdots P_{\Sigma(a)}.

Since
P,P^{\prime}:E
, we can split

P^{\prime}=P^{\prime}_{1}\cdots P^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a)}
so that
P^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{:}E_{i}
for each
i
.
By the induction hypothesis,

\Phi_{m}(U_{i}[E_{i}]\llbracket P^{\prime}_{i}\rrbracket)=(U_{i},E_{i},P^{\prime}_{i})\qquad\mbox{(for each $i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)$).}

Since
|U_{j}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime}_{j}\rrbracket|=|U_{j}[E_{j}]|=|T_{j}|\geq m
and

|U_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j^{\prime}}]\llbracket P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\rrbracket|=|U_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j^{\prime}}]|=|T_{j^{\prime}}|\geq m
for some
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq\Sigma(a)
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(U[E\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket])

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}(a(U_{1}[E_{1}\llbracket P^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket],\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)}[E_{\Sigma(a)}\llbracket P^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a)}\rrbracket]))

\displaystyle=([\,],a(U_{1}[E_{1}],\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)}[E_{\Sigma(a)}]),P^{\prime}_{1}\cdots P^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a)})

\displaystyle=(U,E,P^{\prime})

as required.

•

The second case of Equation (
2
):
We have
|T_{j}|\geq m
for a unique
j\in\{1,\dots,\Sigma(a)\}
and:

n\triangleq|U_{0}|\geq m\qquad U=[\,]\qquad E=\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{1}[E_{j}]\qquad P=U_{0}\cdot P_{j}

for
U_{0}\triangleq a(T_{1},\dots,T_{j-1},U_{j},T_{j+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})
.
Because
|T_{j}|\geq m
, we have
|E_{j}|\geq m
by Lemma
19
.
Now we have
|E_{j}\llbracket P_{j}\rrbracket|=|E_{j}|\geq m
,
|U_{j}|<m
, and
\Phi_{m}(U_{j}[E_{j}\llbracket P_{j}\rrbracket])=\Phi_{m}(T_{j})=(U_{j},E_{j},P_{j})
; hence
by Lemma
20
, we have
\Phi_{m}(E_{j}\llbracket P_{j}\rrbracket)=([\,],E_{j},P_{j})
.
Let
P^{\prime}=U_{0}^{\prime}\cdot P^{\prime\prime}
.
By the assumption,
U_{0}^{\prime}\mathbin{:}\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{1}
and thus
U_{0}^{\prime}
is a
1
-context.
Also,
U_{0}^{\prime}
is good for
m
; thus
|U_{0}^{\prime}|\geq m

and
U^{\prime}_{0}
is of the form
a^{\prime}(T_{1}^{\prime},\dots,T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1},U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},T^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a^{\prime})})

where
|U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}|<m
and
|T^{\prime}_{i}|<m
for every
i\neq j^{\prime}
.
Now we have

(1)

\Phi_{m}(U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]\llbracket P_{j}\rrbracket)=(U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},E_{j},P_{j})
by Lemma
20
and
\Phi_{m}(E_{j}\llbracket P_{j}\rrbracket)=([\,],E_{j},P_{j})
;

(2)

P^{\prime\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E_{j})}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E_{j}{\centerdot}i}
since

P^{\prime}=U_{0}^{\prime}\cdot P^{\prime\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}
where
E=\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{1}[E_{j}]
;

(3)

|U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]|\;<\;|U_{0}^{\prime}[E_{j}]|\;=\;n+|E_{j}|\;=\;|E|\;=\;|U[E]|
.

Therefore, we can apply the induction hypothesis to
U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]
, resulting in

\Phi_{m}(U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket)=(U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},E_{j},P^{\prime\prime}).

We have
|U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]|\geq|E_{j}|\geq m
and
|T^{\prime}_{i}|<m
for every
i\neq j^{\prime}
.
Furthermore

|a^{\prime}(T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1},U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}+1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a^{\prime})})|\;=\;|U_{0}^{\prime}|\;=\;n\;\geq\;m.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(U[E]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}(U_{0}^{\prime}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}(a^{\prime}(T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1},U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}},T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}+1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a^{\prime})})[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}(a^{\prime}(T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1},U^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket,T^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}+1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a^{\prime})}))

\displaystyle=([\,],\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{1}[E_{j}],U^{\prime}_{0}\cdot P^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=(U,E,P^{\prime})

•

The third case of Equation (
2
):
We have
|T_{j}|=|U_{j}[E_{j}]|\geq m
for a unique
j\in\{1,\dots,\Sigma(a)\}
(and thus
|T_{i}|<m
for every
i\neq j
) and:

U=a(T_{1},\dots,T_{j-1},U_{j},T_{j+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})\qquad|U|<m\qquad E=E_{j}\qquad P=P_{j}.

By the induction hypothesis,

\Phi_{m}(U_{j}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(U_{j},E_{j},P^{\prime}).

Since
|T_{i}|<m
for every
i\neq j
and
|a(T_{1},\dots,T_{j-1},U_{j},T_{j+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})|<m
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(U[E]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}(a(T_{1},\dots,T_{j-1},U_{j}[E_{j}]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket,T_{j+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)}))

\displaystyle=(a(T_{1},\dots,T_{j-1},U_{j},T_{j+1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)}),E_{j},P^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(U,E,P^{\prime})

•

The fourth case of Equation (
2
):
Let
n
be
|T|
; we have
n\geq m
and:

U=[\,]\qquad E=\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{0}\qquad P=T.

Since
P^{\prime}:E
,
P^{\prime}
must be a singleton sequence consisting of a tree,
say
T^{\prime}=a^{\prime}(T_{1}^{\prime},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a^{\prime})}^{\prime})
.
By the assumption,
T^{\prime}
is good for
m
.
Hence
|T^{\prime}|\geq m
and
|T_{i}^{\prime}|<m
for every
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a^{\prime})
.
So

\Phi_{m}(U[E]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)\;=\;\Phi_{m}(T^{\prime})\;=\;([\,],\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{0},T^{\prime})\;=\;(U,E,P^{\prime}).

Corollary 22
.

If
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
, then

\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(E\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(E,P^{\prime})
for any
P^{\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}
.

Proof 3.10
.

If
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
, then
(U,E^{\prime},P)=\Phi_{m}(T)
and
U[E^{\prime}]=E
for some
U,E^{\prime}
.
Since
\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E^{\prime})}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E^{\prime}{\centerdot}i}
, Lemma
21

implies
\Phi_{m}(E\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=\Phi_{m}(U[E^{\prime}]\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(U,E^{\prime},P^{\prime})
. Thus, we have

\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(E\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(U[E^{\prime}],P^{\prime})=(E,P^{\prime})
as required.

We are now ready to prove the product lemma.

Lemma 23

(Product Lemma (for Grammar-Independent Decomposition))
.

For
E
and
m\geq 1
,
if
\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
is non-empty, then

\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}.

Proof 3.11
.

The direction
\subseteq
follows from
the fact that
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
implies
P\mathbin{:}E
and
P{\centerdot}i
is good for
m

(Lemma
14
).

We show the other direction.
Since
\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}\neq\emptyset
, there exist
P
and
T
such that
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)=(E,P)
.
Let
P^{\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}
.
By Corollary
22
, we have
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(E\llbracket P^{\prime}\rrbracket)=(E,P^{\prime})
.
This means that
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
.

Lemma
16
follows as
an immediate corollary of Lemmas
18
and
23
.

3.3.
Grammars in Canonical Form

As a preparation for generalizing the decomposition of
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)
(Lemma
16
)
to that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
,
we first transform a given regular tree grammar into
canonical form
, which will be defined
shortly (in Definition
3.3
).
We prove that the transformation preserves unambiguity and (a weaker version of) strong connectivity.

{defi}

[Canonical Grammar]

A rewriting rule of a regular tree grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
is in
canonical form
if it is of the form

N\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}).

A grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
is
canonical
if every rewriting rule is in canonical form.

We transform a given regular tree grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
to
an equivalent one in canonical form.
The idea of the transformation is fairly simple: we replace a rewriting rule

N\longrightarrow a(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})\qquad\mbox{($T_{1},\dots,T_{n}$ are $(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})$-trees)}

such that
T_{i}\notin{\mathcal{N}}
with rules

\{\;\;N\longrightarrow a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},N^{\prime},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{n}),\qquad N^{\prime}\longrightarrow T_{i}\;\;\}

where
N^{\prime}
is a fresh nonterminal that does not appear in
{\mathcal{N}}
.
After iteratively applying the above transformation, we next replace a rewriting rule of the form
N\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
with rules

\{N_{0}\longrightarrow a(\dots,N^{\prime},\dots)\mid(N_{0}\longrightarrow a(\dots,N,\dots))\in{\mathcal{R}}\}.

Again by iteratively applying this transformation, we finally obtain a grammar in canonical form.

The transformation, however, does not preserve strong connectivity.
For example, consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\{\mathtt{a}\mapsto 2,\mathtt{b}\mapsto 1,\mathtt{c}\mapsto 0\},\{N\},{\mathcal{R}})
where

{\mathcal{R}}=\{\;\;N\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\qquad N\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(N,N)\;\;\}.

Then the above transformation introduces a nonterminal
N^{\prime}
as well as rules

N\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(N^{\prime})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad N^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathtt{c}.

Then
N
is not reachable from
N^{\prime}
.

Observe that the problem above was
caused by a newly introduced nonterminal that generates a single finite tree.
To overcome the problem, we introduce a weaker version of strong connectivity
called
essential strong-connectivity
. It
requires strong connectivity only for nonterminals generating infinite languages;
hence, it is preserved by the above transformation.

{defi}
[Essential Strong-connectivity]
Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar.
We say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is
essentially strongly-connected
if for any nonterminals
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}
with
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}\right)\right)=\infty
,
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}
.

Note that by the definition, every strongly-connected grammar is
also essentially-strongly connected.
In the definition above, as well as in the arguments below, nonterminals
N
with
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)=\infty
play an important role.
We write
{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
for the subset of
{\mathcal{N}}
consisting of such nonterminals.

Remark 24
.

A regular tree grammar that is essentially strongly-connected can be easily transformed into a strongly-connected grammar, hence the terminology.
Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be an essentially strongly-connected grammar and
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
We say that a nonterminal is
inessential
if it generates a finite language.
Let
N_{0}
be an inessential nonterminal of a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}\right)=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\}
.
Then by replacing each rule

N_{1}\longrightarrow C[N_{0},\ldots,N_{0}]

(where
C
is a
k
-context possibly having nonterminals other than
N_{0}
) with rules

\{N_{1}\longrightarrow C[T_{i_{1}},\ldots,T_{i_{k}}]\mid i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}\},

one can remove the inessential nonterminal
N_{0}
from the grammar.
A grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected if and only if the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
obtained by removing all inessential nonterminals is strongly-connected.
This transformation preserves the language in the following sense:
writing
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\Sigma,{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
for the resulting grammar,
we have
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N\right)
for each
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
Note that the process of erasing inessential nonterminals breaks canonicity; in fact,
the class of languages generated by strongly-connected canonical regular tree grammars
is a
proper
subset of that of essentially strongly-connected canonical regular tree grammars.

Recall that the second main theorem (Theorem
6
) takes a family
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}

from
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})=\bigcup_{N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
In order to restate the theorem for essentially strongly connected grammars,
we need to replace

{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})
with the “essential” version, namely,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\triangleq\bigcup_{N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right).

Lemma 25

(Canonical Form)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar that is unambiguous and strongly connected.
Then one can (effectively) construct a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
and
a family
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
of subsets
\mathcal{I}_{N}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.

•

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\biguplus_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

If
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
, then
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.

Proof 3.12
.

See Appendix
B
.

3.4.
Decomposition of Regular Tree Languages

This subsection generalizes the decomposition of
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)

in Section
3.2
:

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\Sigma)\;\cong\coprod_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}

to
that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, and proves a bijection of the following form:

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\;\cong\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}}).

Here,
\widetilde{E}
denotes a
typed
second-order context
(which
will be defined shortly in Section
3.4.1
),
each of whose second-order holes

\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}
carries not only the size
n
but the
context type

\kappa
of a context to be substituted for the hole.
Accordingly, we have replaced

\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})
, which denotes the set of contexts that respect
the context type specified by
\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i
.

In the rest of this subsection, we first define the notion of typed second-order contexts in
Section
3.4.1
, extend the decomposition function accordingly in Section
3.4.2
, and use it to prove the above bijection in Section
3.4.3
.
Throughout this subsection (i.e., Section
3.4
),
we assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a canonical and unambiguous grammar.
We emphasize here that the discussion in this subsection
essentially relies on both unambiguity and canonicity of the grammar.
The essential strong connectivity is not required for the results in this
subsection; it will be used in Section
3.5
, to show
that each component
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})
contains an affine
context that has
S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
as a subcontext
(recall condition (i) of (T3) in Section
3.1
).

Remark 26
.

Note that any deterministic bottom-up tree automaton
(without any
\epsilon
-rules)
[
21
]

can be considered an unambiguous canonical tree grammar, by regarding
each transition rule
a(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\longrightarrow q
as a rewriting rule

N_{q}\longrightarrow a(N_{q_{1}},\ldots,N_{q_{k}})
. Thus, by the equivalence between the
class of tree languages generated by regular tree grammars and the class of those
accepted by deterministic bottom-up tree automata, any regular tree grammar can be
converted to an unambiguous canonical tree grammar.

3.4.1.
Typed Second-Order Contexts

The set of
{\mathcal{G}}
-typed second-order contexts
, ranged over by
\widetilde{E}
, is defined by:

\widetilde{E}::=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]\;\mid\;a(\widetilde{E}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{E}_{\Sigma(a)})\ \ (a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Sigma)).

The subscript
\kappa=(N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N)
describes the type of
first-order contexts that can be substituted for this second-order hole;
the superscript
n
describes the size as before.
Hence a (first-order) context
C
is suitable for
filling a second-order hole
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}
if
C:\kappa
and
|C|=n
.
We write
C\mathbin{:}\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}
if
C:\kappa
and
|C|=n
.
The operations such as
\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i
,
\widetilde{E}\llbracket C\rrbracket
and
\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket
are defined
analogously.
For a sequence of contexts
P=C_{1}C_{2}\cdots C_{\ell}
,
we write
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}

if
\#\!\left(P\right)(=\ell)=\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})
and
C_{i}\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i
for each
i\leq\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})
.

We define the second-order context typing relation
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
inductively
by the rules in Figure
5
. Intuitively,

\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
means that
\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
holds for any
P
such that
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
(as confirmed in Lemma
28
below).
As in the case of untyped second-order contexts,
we actually use only typed second-order contexts with holes
of the form
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}
where
k
is
0
or
1
.

\begin{array}[]{cr}\dfrac{\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}\quad\mbox{(for each $i=1,\dots,k$)}}{\vdash\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]:N}&(\text{SC-Hole})\\[25.0pt]
\dfrac{(N\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}))\in{\mathcal{R}}\qquad\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}\quad\mbox{(for each $i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)$)}}{\vdash a(\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{\Sigma(a)}):N}&(\text{SC-Term})\end{array}

Figure 5
.
Typing rules for
\widetilde{E}

{exa}

Recall the second-order context

E=\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{1}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{0}^{5}))])
in
Figure
4
and Example
3.2.1
.
Given the grammar consisting of the rules:

A\to\mathtt{a}(B,B)\qquad B\to\mathtt{b}(A)\qquad B\to\mathtt{b}(B)\qquad B\to\mathtt{c},

the corresponding
typed
second-order context
\widetilde{E}
is:

\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{A{\Rightarrow}A}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{{\Rightarrow}B}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{{\Rightarrow}A}^{5}))])

and we have
\vdash\widetilde{E}:B
.
For
P=\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c})\cdot\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))
, we have
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
,
and:

\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))))),\mathtt{c}))\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},B\right).

Lemma 27
.

The following rule is derivable:

\begin{array}[]{cr}\dfrac{C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)\qquad\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}\quad\mbox{\rm(for each $i=1,\dots,k$)}}{\vdash C[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]:N}&(\mbox{\rm SC-Ctx})\end{array}

Proof 3.13
.

The proof proceeds by induction on
C
.
Assume that the premises of the rule hold.
If
C=[\,]
, then
C[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]=\widetilde{E}_{1}

and
N=N_{1}
; thus the result follows immediately from the assumption.
If
C=a(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
, then by the assumption that the grammar is canonical
and
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
,
we have
N\to a(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{\ell})
with
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{k_{i-1}+1}\dots N_{k_{i}}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}_{i}\right)

for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
where
k_{0}=0
and
k_{\ell}=k
.
By the induction hypothesis,
we have
\vdash C_{i}[\widetilde{E}_{k_{i-1}+1},\ldots,\widetilde{E}_{k_{i}}]:N^{\prime}_{i}
.
Thus, by using rule (
SC-Term), we have

\vdash C[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]:N
as required.

Lemma 28
.

Assume that
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
.

(1)

If
\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})=0
, then
\widetilde{E}
is a tree and
\widetilde{E}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

(2)

If
\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})\geq 1
and
C\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}1
, then
\vdash\widetilde{E}\llbracket C\rrbracket:N
.

(3)

If
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
, then
\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

Proof 3.14
.

(1)
By induction on the structure of
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
.

(2)
By induction on the structure of
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
.
The base case is when
\widetilde{E}=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]
.
Since
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
, we have
\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}
for each
i
.
Then by the derived rule (
SC-Ctx
), we have
\vdash C[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]:N
.

(3)
By induction on
\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})
(using
(1)
and
(2)
).

3.4.2.
Grammar-Respecting Decomposition Function
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}

We have defined in Section
3.2.2

the function
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
that decomposes a tree
T
and
returns a pair
(E,P)
of
a second-order context
E
and
a sufficiently long sequence
P
of (first-order) contexts.
The aim here is to extend
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}
to a grammar-respecting one

\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}
that takes a pair
(T,N)
such that

T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
as an input, and returns a pair
(\widetilde{E},P)
,
which is the same as
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)=(E,P)
, except that

\widetilde{E}
is a “type-annotated” version of
E
.
For example, for the tree
in Figure
4
and the grammar in Example
3.4.1
,
we expect that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T)=(\widetilde{E},P)
where:

\begin{array}[]{l}T=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))))),\mathtt{c}))\\
\widetilde{E}=\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{A{\Rightarrow}A}^{3}[\mathtt{a}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{{\Rightarrow}B}^{3},\mathtt{b}(\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{{\Rightarrow}A}^{5}))])\qquad P=\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}([\,]),\mathtt{c})\cdot\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}))\cdot\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c}),\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{c})).\end{array}

We say that
\widetilde{E}
refines
E
, written
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
, if
E
is obtained by simply forgetting type annotations, i.e., replacing
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}
in
\widetilde{E}
with
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}
.
This relation is formally defined by induction on the structures of
E
and
\widetilde{E}
by the rules in Figure
6
.

\begin{array}[]{cr}\dfrac{\widetilde{E}_{i}\lhd E_{i}\quad\mbox{(for each $i=1,\dots,k$)}}{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]\lhd\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}[E_{1},\dots,E_{n}]}&(\text{Ref-Hole})\\[25.0pt]
\dfrac{\widetilde{E}_{i}\lhd E_{i}\quad\mbox{(for each $i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)$)}}{a(\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{\Sigma(a)})\lhd a(E_{1},\dots,E_{n})}&(\text{Ref-Term})\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Refinement relation

The following lemma is obtained by straightforward induction on
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
.

Lemma 29
.

Assume
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
.

(1)

|\widetilde{E}|=|E|
.

(2)

If
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
, then
P\mathbin{:}E
.

(3)

If
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
, then
\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket=E\llbracket P\rrbracket
.

Given
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
and
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
,
the value of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)
should be
(\widetilde{E},P)

where
\widetilde{E}
is a
{\mathcal{G}}
-typed second-order context that satisfies the following conditions:

\widetilde{E}\lhd E,\qquad\vdash\widetilde{E}:N\quad\mbox{and}\quad P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}.

We prove that, for every
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
,
there exists exactly one typed second-order context
\widetilde{E}
that satisfies the above condition.
Hence the above constraints define the function
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)
.

We first state and prove a similar
result for first-order contexts (in Lemma
30
),
and then prove the second-order version (in Lemma
31
),
using the former.

Lemma 30
.

Let
C
be a (first-order)
k
-context and
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
be trees.
Assume that
C[T_{1},\dots,T_{k}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.
Then there exists a unique family
(N_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}
such that

C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
and
T_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}\right)
for every
i=1,\dots,k
.

Proof 3.15
.

By induction on
C
.

•

Case
C=[\,]
:
Then
k=1
.
The existence follows from
[\,]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
(as
N\longrightarrow^{*}N
) and
T_{1}=C[T_{1}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.
The uniqueness follows from the fact that
[\,]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
implies
N_{1}=N
.

•

Case
C=a(C_{1},\dots,C_{\Sigma(a)})
:
Let
\ell=\Sigma(a)
,
k_{i}=\mathtt{hn}(C_{i})
for each
i=1,\dots,\ell
and

(T_{1,1},\dots,T_{1,k_{1}},T_{2,1},\dots,T_{2,k_{2}},\dots,T_{\ell,1},\dots,T_{\ell,k_{\ell}})=(T_{1},\dots,T_{k}).

Then
C[T_{1},\dots,T_{k}]=a(C_{1}[T_{1,1},\dots,T_{1,k}],\dots,C_{\ell}[T_{\ell,1},\dots T_{\ell,k_{\ell}}])
.
Since
C[T_{1},\dots,T_{k}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, there exists a rewriting sequence

N\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\ell})\longrightarrow^{*}a(C_{1}[T_{1,1},\dots,T_{1,k_{1}}],\dots,C_{\ell}[T_{\ell,1},\dots T_{\ell,k_{\ell}}]).

Thus
N_{i}\longrightarrow^{*}C_{i}[T_{i,1},\dots,T_{i,k_{i}}]
, i.e.,
C_{i}[T_{i,1},\dots,T_{i,k_{i}}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}\right)
, for each
i=1,\dots,\ell
.
By the induction hypothesis, there exist
N_{i,1},\dots,N_{i,k_{i}}
such that
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i,1}\dots N_{i,k_{i}}{\Rightarrow}N_{i}\right)
and
T_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i,j}\right)
for each
j=1,\dots,k_{i}
.
Then (the rearrangement of) the family
(N_{i,j})_{i\leq\ell,j\leq k_{i}}
satisfies the requirement.

We prove the uniqueness.
Assume that both
(N^{1}_{i,j})_{i,j}
and
(N^{2}_{i,j})_{i,j}
satisfy the requirement.
Then, for each
m=1,2
, there exist
N^{m}_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,\ell
) such that

\displaystyle N\longrightarrow a(N^{m}_{1},\dots,N^{m}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}a(C_{1}[N^{m}_{1,1},\dots,N^{m}_{1,k_{1}}],\dots,C_{\ell}[N^{m}_{\ell,1},\dots,N^{m}_{\ell,k_{\ell}}])

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}a(C_{1}[T_{1,1},\dots,T_{1,k_{1}}],\dots,C_{\ell}[T_{\ell,1},\dots,T_{\ell,k_{\ell}}]).

Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is unambiguous,
N^{1}_{i}=N^{2}_{i}
for each
i=1,\dots,\ell
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have
N^{1}_{i,j}=N^{2}_{i,j}
for each
i
and
j
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a canonical unambiguous regular tree grammar and
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.
Given
E
and
P
, assume that
P\mathbin{:}E
and
E\llbracket P\rrbracket=T
.
Then
E
has a unique refinement
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
such that
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
.

Proof 3.16
.

We prove by induction on
E
.

•

Case
E=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{k}^{n}[E_{1},\dots,E_{k}]
:
The sequence
P
can be decomposed as
P=C\cdot P_{1}\cdots P_{k}
so that
P_{i}\mathbin{:}E_{i}
for each
i=1,\dots,k
.
Furthermore
\mathtt{hn}(C)=k
and
|C|=n
.
We have
E\llbracket P\rrbracket=C[E_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,E_{k}\llbracket P_{k}\rrbracket]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

We prove the existence.
By Lemma
30
, there exists a family
(N_{i})_{i=1,\dots,k}
such that
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
(i.e.,
C:\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}
) and
E_{i}\llbracket P_{i}\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}\right)
for each
i=1,\dots,k
.
By the induction hypothesis, for each
i=1,\dots,k
, there exists
\widetilde{E}_{i}\lhd E_{i}
such that
\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}
and
P_{i}\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}_{i}
.
Let
\widetilde{E}\triangleq\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{k}]
.
Then
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
,
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
, and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
.

We prove the uniqueness.
Assume
\widetilde{E}^{1}
and
\widetilde{E}^{2}
satisfy that
\widetilde{E}^{j}\lhd E
,
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}:N
and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}^{j}
for
j=1,2
.
Since
\widetilde{E}^{j}\lhd E
and
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}:N
,
\widetilde{E}^{j}
must be of the form:
\widetilde{E}^{j}=\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{N_{1}^{j}\dots N_{k}^{j}{\Rightarrow}N}^{n}[\widetilde{E}^{j}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}^{j}_{k}]
with
\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}\lhd E_{i}
and
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}:N^{j}_{i}
.
Since
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}^{j}
, we have
C\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}^{j}\dots N_{k}^{j}{\Rightarrow}N\right)

and
P_{i}:\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
.
By Lemma
28
,

\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}\llbracket P_{i}\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}^{j}\right)
for each
i=1,\dots,k
and
j=1,2
.
By Lemma
29
(
3
),

E\llbracket P\rrbracket=\widetilde{E}^{j}\llbracket P\rrbracket=C[\widetilde{E}^{j}_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,\widetilde{E}^{j}_{k}\llbracket P_{k}\rrbracket]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.
Hence by Lemma
30
,
N_{i}^{1}=N_{i}^{2}
for each
i=1,\dots,k
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\widetilde{E}_{i}^{1}=\widetilde{E}_{i}^{2}
for each
i
.
Hence
\widetilde{E}^{1}=\widetilde{E}^{2}
.

•

Case
E=a(E_{1},\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)})
:
The sequence
P
can be decomposed as
P=P_{1}\cdots P_{\Sigma}(a)
so that
P_{i}\mathbin{:}E_{i}
for each
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
Then
E\llbracket P\rrbracket=a(E_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)}\llbracket P_{\Sigma(a)}\rrbracket)
.

We prove the existence.
Since
N\longrightarrow^{*}a(E_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)}\llbracket P_{\Sigma(a)}\rrbracket)
,
there exists a rule
N\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)})
such that

N\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow^{*}a(E_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)}\llbracket P_{\Sigma(a)}\rrbracket).

So
N_{i}\longrightarrow^{*}E_{i}\llbracket P_{i}\rrbracket
for each
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
By the induction hypothesis, there exists
\widetilde{E}_{i}\lhd E_{i}
such that
\vdash\widetilde{E}_{i}:N_{i}
and
P_{i}\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}_{i}
.
Let
\widetilde{E}\triangleq a(\widetilde{E}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}_{\Sigma(a)})
.
Then
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
,
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
, and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
.

We prove the uniqueness.
Assume
\widetilde{E}^{1}
and
\widetilde{E}^{2}
satisfy that
\widetilde{E}^{j}\lhd E
,
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}:N
and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}^{j}
for
j=1,2
.
Since
\widetilde{E}^{j}\lhd E
,
\widetilde{E}^{j}
must be of the form:

\widetilde{E}^{j}=a(\widetilde{E}^{j}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{E}^{j}_{\Sigma(a)})
with
\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}\lhd E_{i}
.
By
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}:N
, there exists a rule
N\longrightarrow a(N_{1}^{j},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}^{j})

such that
\vdash\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}:N_{i}^{j}
for each
i
.
Since
P:\widetilde{E}^{j}
, we have
P_{i}:\widetilde{E}^{j}_{i}
for each
i
.
By Lemmas
28
and
29
(
3
),
we have

\widetilde{E}_{i}^{j}\llbracket P_{i}\rrbracket=E_{i}\llbracket P_{i}\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}^{j}\right)
.
Now we have

N\longrightarrow a(N_{1}^{j},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}^{j})\longrightarrow^{*}a(E_{1}\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,E_{\Sigma(a)}\llbracket P_{\Sigma(a)}\rrbracket)

for
j=1,2
.
Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is unambiguous,
N_{i}^{1}=N_{i}^{2}
for each
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\widetilde{E}_{i}^{1}=\widetilde{E}_{i}^{2}
for each
i
.
Hence
\widetilde{E}^{1}=\widetilde{E}^{2}
.

3.4.3.
Decomposition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)

To formally state the decomposition lemma, we prepare some definitions.
For a canonical unambiguous grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
,
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
n\geq 1
, and
m\geq 1
,
we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}}({\mathcal{G}})
by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{\widetilde{E}\mid(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)\text{ for some }T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\text{ and }P\}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{P\mid(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)\text{ for some }T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}}({\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\{U\mid U:\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n},U\text{ is good for }m\}.

The set
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
consists of second-order contexts that are obtained by
decomposing trees of size
n
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
consists of
affine context sequences that match
\widetilde{E}
. The set
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
is the set of contexts
that match the hole
\llbracket\,\rrbracket_{\kappa}^{n}
.

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 32
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\quad\cong

\displaystyle\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}}).

The lemma above
is a direct consequence of
typed versions of coproduct and product lemmas (Lemmas
33
and

35
below).
The following coproduct lemma can be shown in a manner similar to Lemma
18
:

Lemma 33

(Coproduct Lemma (for Grammar-Respecting Decomposition))
.

For any

n\geq 1
and
m\geq 1
,
there exists a bijection

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\quad\cong

\displaystyle\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N).

such that
(\widetilde{E},P)
in the right hand side is mapped to
\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket
.

Proof 3.17
.

We define a function

f:\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)

by
f(\widetilde{E},P)\triangleq\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket
,
and a function

g:{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\longrightarrow\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)

by
g(T)\triangleq\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)
.

Let us check that these are functions into the codomains:

•

f(\widetilde{E},P)\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
:
Since
P\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
, there exists
T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
such that
(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)
.
By Lemmas
14
and
29
, we have
f(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket=T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

•

g(T)\in\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
:
Obvious from the definitions of
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
.

We have
f(g(T))=T
by Lemmas
14
(
1
) and
29
.
Let
(\widetilde{E},P)\in\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
.
By definition, there exists
T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
such that
(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}{(T,N)}=g(T)
.
Then

g(f(\widetilde{E},P))=g(f(g(T))=g(T)=(\widetilde{E},P).

The following is a key lemma used for proving a typed version of the product lemma.

Lemma 34
.

For
a nonterminal
N
,

n\geq 1
,
m\geq 1
and
\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
, let
E
be the unique second-order context such that
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)=\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}\cap\{P\mid P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}\}.

Proof 3.18
.

The direction
\subseteq
is clear.
We prove the converse.

Let
P
be in the right hand side.
Since
\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
, there exist
T^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
such that

\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T^{\prime},N)=(\widetilde{E},P^{\prime}).

By the definition of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T^{\prime},N)
, we have
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
.
Since
P\in\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}
, there exists
T
such that
(E,P)=\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(T)
and thus, by Lemma
14
,

\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(E\llbracket P\rrbracket)=(E,P).

Since
\Phi_{m}^{\bullet}(E\llbracket P\rrbracket)=(E,P)
,
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
,
\vdash\widetilde{E}:N
,
and
P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}
, by the definition of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}
, we have
\widetilde{\Phi}_{m}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(E\llbracket P\rrbracket,N)=(\widetilde{E},P)
.
By Lemmas
28
(
3
)
and
29
(
3
),
E\llbracket P\rrbracket=\widetilde{E}\llbracket P\rrbracket\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.
Hence
P\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
.

The following is the typed version of the product lemma, which follows from
Lemmas
23
and
34
.

Lemma 35

(Product Lemma (for Grammar-Respecting Decomposition))
.

For
any nonterminal
N
,

n\geq 1
,

m\geq 1
and

\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}}).

Proof 3.19
.

Let
E
be the unique second-order context such that
\widetilde{E}\lhd E
.
By Lemmas
23
and
34
, we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\widetilde{E}}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{E}^{m}\cap\{P\mid P\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}\}

\displaystyle=(\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(E)}\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i})\cap(\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\{U\mid U\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i\})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}(\mathcal{U}^{m}_{E{\centerdot}i}\cap\{U\mid U\mathbin{:}\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i\})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}}).

Lemma
32
is an immediate
corollary of Lemmas
33
and
35
.

3.5.
Each Component Contains the Subcontext of Interest

In Section
3.4
,
we have shown that the set
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
of trees can be decomposed as:

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\quad\cong\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}}),

assuming that
{\mathcal{G}}
is canonical and unambiguous.
In this subsection, we further assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly connected,
and prove that, for each tree context
S\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
, every component “contains”
S
, i.e., there exists
U\in\;\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})
such that

S\preceq U

if
m
is sufficiently large, say
m\geq m_{0}
(where
m_{0}
depends on
|S|
).
More precisely, the goal of this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 36
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be an unambiguous, essentially strongly-connected grammar in canonical form and
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of linear contexts in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
such that
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exist integers
b,c\geq 1
that satisfy the following:
For any
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,
n\geq 1
,
m\geq b
,
\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{cm}({\mathcal{G}},N)

and
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})\}
,
there exists
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{cm}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})
such that
S_{m}\preceq U
.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to a proof of the lemma above.
The idea of the proof is as follows.
Assume
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
(
N_{1},N_{2}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
) and
\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{\kappa}=\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i
.
Recall that

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n}_{\kappa}}({\mathcal{G}})=\{U\mid U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right),\;|U|=n,\mbox{ and $U$ is good for $m$}\}.

It is not difficult to find a context
U
that satisfies both
U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
and
S\preceq U
.
For example, assume that
\kappa=N_{0}{\Rightarrow}N_{3}
(
N_{0},N_{3}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
).
Then, since the grammar is assumed to be essentially strongly-connected,
there exist
S_{0,1}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)
and
S_{2,3}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N_{3}\right)

and then
U\triangleq S_{2,3}[S[S_{0,1}]]
satisfies
U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
and
S\preceq U
.
What is relatively difficult is to show that
S_{2,3}
and
S_{0,1}
can be chosen so that they meet the required size constraints (i.e.,
|U|=n
and
U
is good for
m
).

The following is a key lemma, which states
that any essentially strongly connected grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
is

periodic
in the sense that there is a constant
c
(that depends on
{\mathcal{G}})

and a family of constants
d_{N,N^{\prime}}
such that,
for each
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,
and sufficiently large
n
,

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
if and only if

n\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime}}\mod c
.

Lemma 37
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar.
Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected and
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Then there exist constants
n_{0},c>0
and a family
(d_{N,N^{\prime}})_{N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}}
of natural numbers
0\leq d_{N,N^{\prime}}<c
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

For every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
, if
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
, then
n\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime}}\mod c
.

(2)

The converse of (
1
) holds for sufficiently large
n
:
for any
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and
n\geq n_{0}
,
if
n\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime}}\mod c
then
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
.

(3)

d_{N,N}=0
for every
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.

(4)

d_{N,N^{\prime}}+d_{N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}}\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime\prime}}\mod c
for every
N,N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.

The proof of the above lemma is rather involved;
we defer it to Appendix
C
.
We give some examples below, to clarify what the lemma means.

{exa}

Consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
consisting of the following rewriting rules:

A\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(B)\qquad B\longrightarrow\mathtt{b}(A)\qquad A\longrightarrow\mathtt{c}(C)\qquad C\longrightarrow\mathtt{c}

Then
{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}=\{A,B\}
and the conditions of the lemma above hold for:

n_{0}=0\qquad c=2\qquad d_{A,A}=d_{B,B}=0\qquad d_{A,B}=d_{B,A}=1.

In fact,
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{1},A{\Rightarrow}A\right)=\{(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b})^{k}[\,]\mid k\geq 0\}
,

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{1},B{\Rightarrow}B\right)=\{(\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a})^{k}[\,]\mid k\geq 0\}
,

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{1},B{\Rightarrow}A\right)=\{(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b})^{k}\mathtt{a}[\,]\mid k\geq 0\}
,
and

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{1},A{\Rightarrow}B\right)=\{(\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a})^{k}\mathtt{b}[\,]\mid k\geq 0\}
.
Here, since the arities of
\mathtt{a}
and
\mathtt{b}
are
1
, we have
used regular expressions to denote linear contexts.

Consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
,
obtained by adding the following rules to the grammar above.

A\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(A_{1})\qquad A_{1}\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(A_{2})\qquad A_{2}\longrightarrow\mathtt{a}(A).

Then, the conditions of the lemma above hold for

n=6
,
c=1
,
d_{N,N^{\prime}}=0
for
N,N^{\prime}\in\{A,A_{1},A_{2},B\}
. Note that

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}_{2},A_{2}{\Rightarrow}A_{1}\right)=\{\mathtt{a}^{2}(\mathtt{a}^{3}|\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b})^{k}\mathtt{a}^{2}[\,]\mid k\geq 0\}
.
∎

Remark 38
.

With some additional assumptions on a grammar,
Lemma
37
above can be easily proved.
For example, consider a canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected grammar
{\mathcal{G}}

and assume that
(i)
{\mathcal{G}}
is
N
-aperiodic for some
N

and (ii)
there exist a
2
-context
C
and nonterminals
N,N_{1},N_{2}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
such that
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}C[N_{1},N_{2}]
.
Then Lemma
37
for the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
trivially holds with
c=1
.
In fact, this simpler approach is essentially what we adopted in the conference version
[
18
]
of this article.

Using the lemma above, we prove that
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
and any sufficiently large

n
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset
,
we can find a context
U\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
such that
S\preceq U

(Lemma
39
below).

Lemma 39
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar in canonical form.
Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected and
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Then there exists a constant
n_{0}\in{\rm Nature}
that satisfies the following condition:
For every

•

S\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
,

•

n\geq n_{0}+|S|
, and

•

\kappa=({}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
or
(N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
where
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,

if
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset
, then there exists
U\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
with
S\preceq U
.

Proof 3.20
.

First, let us choose the following constants:

•

m_{0}\in{\rm Nature}
such that, for every
N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,
there exists
S_{N,N^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
with
|S_{N,N^{\prime}}|\leq m_{0}
.
The existence of
m_{0}
is a consequence of essential strong-connectivity and of finiteness of
{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

m_{1}
which is the constant
n_{0}
of Lemma
37
.

•

m_{2}\in{\rm Nature}
such that, for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}

and every
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
, we have
|T|<m_{2}
.
The existence of
m_{2}
follows from the fact that

\bigcup_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
is a finite set.

Let
c

and
(d_{N,N^{\prime}})_{N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}}
be the constant and the family obtained by Lemma
37
.

We define
n_{1}\triangleq m_{0}+m_{1}
and
n_{2}\triangleq m_{0}+m_{1}+rm_{2}+1
, where
r\triangleq\max(\mathrm{Im}\left(\Sigma\right))
.
Below we shall show:
(i) the current lemma for the case
\kappa=(N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
, by setting

n_{0}\triangleq n_{1}

and then (ii) the lemma for the case
\kappa=({\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
,
by setting

n_{0}\triangleq n_{2}
;
we use (i) to show (ii).
The whole lemma
then follows immediately from (i) and (ii) with
n_{0}\triangleq\max\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\,(=n_{2})
.

•

Case (i):
We define
n_{0}\triangleq n_{1}=m_{0}+m_{1}
.
Assume that:
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
where
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
;

n\geq n_{0}+|S|
;
\kappa=(N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
where
N,N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
;
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset
.
Let
S_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
with
|S_{1}|\leq m_{0}
.
Then
S_{1}[S]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
It suffices to show that
{\mathcal{L}}_{n-|S_{1}[S]|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)\neq\emptyset
.
By Lemma
37
(
1
)(
4
),

\mspace{20mu}\begin{aligned} n&\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime}}\equiv d_{N,N_{1}}+d_{N_{1},N_{2}}+d_{N_{2},N^{\prime}}&&\bmod c&&(\because{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)={\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset)\\
|S_{1}|&\equiv d_{N_{2},N^{\prime}}&&\bmod c&&(\because(S_{1}\in)\,{\mathcal{L}}_{|S_{1}|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset)\\
|S|&\equiv d_{N_{1},N_{2}}&&\bmod c&&(\because(S\in)\,{\mathcal{L}}_{|S|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)\neq\emptyset)\end{aligned}

and thus

n-|S_{1}[S]|\equiv d_{N,N_{1}}\mod c.

Since
n\geq m_{0}+m_{1}+|S|
, we have
n-|S_{1}[S]|\geq m_{1}
,
and hence by Lemma
37
(
2
), we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{n-|S_{1}[S]|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)\neq\emptyset
.

•

Case (ii):
We define
n_{0}\triangleq n_{2}=m_{0}+m_{1}+rm_{2}+1
.
Assume that:
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
where
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
;

n\geq n_{0}+|S|
;
\kappa=({}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime})
where
N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
;
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset
.
Let
T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
.
Since arities of terminal symbols are bounded by
r
and
|T|\geq rm_{2}+1
,
there exists a subtree
T_{0}\preceq T
such that
m_{2}\leq|T_{0}|\leq rm_{2}+1
,
which can be shown by induction on tree
T
.
Let
U
be a linear context such that
T=U[T_{0}]
.
Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is canonical and unambiguous, by Lemma
30
,

U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
and
T_{0}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
for some
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.
Since
m_{2}\leq|T_{0}|\leq rm_{2}+1
, we have
N\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and
|U|\geq m_{0}+m_{1}+|S|
.
By using the case
(1)
, since
(U\in)\,{\mathcal{L}}_{|U|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
,
there exists
U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{|U|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
with
S\preceq U^{\prime}
.
Let
T^{\prime}\triangleq U^{\prime}[T_{0}]
; then
T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
and
S\preceq T^{\prime}
.

We prepare another lemma.

Lemma 40
.

If
\Phi_{m}(T)=(U,E,P)
,
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\mathtt{shn}(E)\}
,

P{\centerdot}\ell
is a linear context, and
the second-order hole
E{\centerdot}\ell
occurs in
E
in the form
(E{\centerdot}\ell)[E^{\prime}]
,
then
|E^{\prime}|\geq m
.

Proof 3.21
.

By straightforward induction on
|T|
and case analysis of
Equation (
2
) in the definition of
\Phi_{m}
.
We can use Lemma
19
in the second case of
Equation (
2
) when
\ell=1
;
all the other cases immediately follow from induction hypothesis.

We are now ready to prove the main lemma of this subsection.

Proof 3.22

(Proof of Lemma
36
)
.

Since
S_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
, we have

{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}\neq\emptyset
and hence
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Let
n_{0}
be the constant of Lemma
39
.
Let
c_{1}
be a positive integer such that
|S_{n}|\leq c_{1}n
for every
n\in{\rm Nature}
.
We define
c\triangleq(2r+1)c_{1}
(recall that

r
is the largest arity of
\Sigma
) and choose
b
so that
b\geq n_{0}
and

b>|T|
for any
T\in\bigcup_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}\backslash{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

Assume that
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,
n\geq 1
,
m\geq b
,
\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{cm}({\mathcal{G}},N)

and
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})\}
.
Let
\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i=\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n^{\prime}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.
We need to show that there exists

U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{cm}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n^{\prime}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{G}})
such that
S_{m}\preceq U
.

Since
\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{cm}({\mathcal{G}},N)
,
there exist
T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
and
P
such that

(\widetilde{E},P)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{cm}^{\bullet,{\mathcal{G}}}(T,N)
;
hence
P{\centerdot}i\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{cm}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{cm}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n^{\prime}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{G}})
.
The affine context
P{\centerdot}i
must be of the form

a(U_{1},\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)})
. Since
P{\centerdot}i
is good for
cm
,
we have
|P{\centerdot}i|\geq cm=(2r+1)c_{1}m\geq 2rc_{1}m+1
.
Since
\Sigma(a)\leq r
, there exists
j\leq\Sigma(a)
such that

|U_{j}|\geq 2c_{1}m
.
We have
\kappa^{\prime}=(N^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime\prime})
or
({\Rightarrow}N^{\prime\prime})
for some
N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}
.
Since
P{\centerdot}i=a(U_{1},\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)})\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa^{\prime}\right)
, there exist
N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}\in{\mathcal{N}}
such that

N^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow^{*}T^{\prime}

where
T^{\prime}=P{\centerdot}i[N^{\prime}]
if
\kappa^{\prime}=(N^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime\prime})
,
and
T^{\prime}=P{\centerdot}i
if
\kappa^{\prime}=({\Rightarrow}N^{\prime\prime})
.
Let
\kappa
be
N^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N_{j}
if
U_{j}
is a linear context, and
{\Rightarrow}N_{j}
otherwise.
Then
U_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{|U_{j}|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset
.
In order to apply Lemma
39
(for
S=S_{m}
and
n=|U_{j}|
),
we need to check the conditions: (i)
|U_{j}|\geq n_{0}+|S_{m}|

and (ii)
\kappa
consists of only nonterminals in
{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
Condition (i) follows immediately from
|U_{j}|\geq 2c_{1}m\geq m+c_{1}m\geq n_{0}+|S_{m}|
. As for (ii), it suffices to check that

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{j}\right)
and
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)

contain a tree whose size is no less than
b

(where the condition on
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)
is required only if

U_{j}
is a linear context).
The condition on
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{j}\right)
follows
from
|U_{j}|\geq 2c_{1}m\geq m\geq b
.
If

U_{j}
is a linear context,
by Lemma
40
,
there exist
S
and
T^{\prime\prime}

such that:

T=S[(P{\centerdot}i)[T^{\prime\prime}]]\qquad|T^{\prime\prime}|\geq cm\geq b\qquad T^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)

as required.

Thus, we can apply Lemma
39

and obtain
U_{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)
such that
S_{m}\preceq U_{j}^{\prime}
and
|U_{j}^{\prime}|=|U_{j}|
.
Since
P{\centerdot}i=a(U_{1},\ldots,U_{\Sigma(a)})
is good for
cm
,

U\triangleq a(U_{1},\dots,U_{j-1},U^{\prime}_{j},U_{j+1},\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)})
is also good for
cm
.
Obviously
|U|=|P{\centerdot}i|
and thus
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{cm}_{\llbracket\,\rrbracket^{n^{\prime}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{G}})
.
Since
S_{m}\preceq U_{j}^{\prime}
and
U_{j}^{\prime}\preceq U
, we have
S_{m}\preceq U
as required.

3.6.
Main Proof

Here, we give a proof of Theorem
6
.
Before the proof, we prepare a simple lemma.
We write
\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})
for the set of affine contexts over
\Sigma
of size at most
n
.
Lemma
41
below gives an upper bound of
\#\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})\right)
.
A more precise bound can be obtained by using a technique of analytic combinatorics
such as Drmota–Lalley–Woods theorem (
cf.
[
5
]
, Theorem VII.6),
but the rough bound provided by the lemma below is sufficient for our purpose.

Lemma 41
.

For every ranked alphabet
\Sigma
, there exists a real constant
\gamma>1
such that

\#\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})\right)\leq\gamma^{n}

for every
n\geq 0
.

Proof 3.23
.

Let
A
be the set of symbols:

\{\grave{a},\acute{a}\mid a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Sigma)\}\cup\{\,\square\}
.
Intuitively
\grave{a}
and
\acute{a}
are opening and closing tags of an XML-like language.

We can transform an affine context
U
to its XML-like string representation
U^{\dagger}\in A^{*}
by:

\displaystyle(a(U_{1},\dots,U_{\Sigma(a)}))^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\triangleq\grave{a}\,U_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots U_{\Sigma(a)}^{\dagger}\,\acute{a}

\displaystyle[\,]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\triangleq\square.

Obviously,
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
is injective. Furthermore,
|U^{\dagger}|=2|U|
if
U
is a

0
-context (i.e., a tree), and
|U^{\dagger}|=2|U|+1
if
U
is a linear context
(note that the size of the hole
[\,]
is zero, but its word representation is of length
1
).
Thus, for
n>0
, we have

\#\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})\right)\leq\sum_{i=0}^{2n+1}(\#\!\left(A\right))^{i}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{3n}(\#\!\left(A\right))^{i}\leq(\#\!\left(A\right)+1)^{3n}=((\#\!\left(A\right)+1)^{3})^{n}.

If
n=0
, then
\#\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})\right)=1=((\#\!\left(A\right)+1)^{3})^{n}
,
as
\mathcal{U}_{\leq n}({\Sigma})
is the singleton set
\{[\,]\}
.
Thus, the required result holds for
\gamma=(\#\!\left(A\right)+1)^{3}
.

The following lemma is a variant of
Theorem
6
, specialized to a canonical grammar.

Lemma 42
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a canonical, unambiguous, and essentially strongly-connected regular tree grammar
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
,
and
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of linear contexts in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})

such that
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exists a real constant
p>0
such that
for any
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1.

Proof 3.24
.

The overall structure of the proof is the same as that of Proposition
12
.
Let
Z_{n}
be

1-\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\not\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}.

It suffices to show that
Z_{n}
converges to
0
.

By Lemma
32
,
we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\cong\coprod_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})

for any
m>0
.
Thus, we have

Z_{n}\leq\frac{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\{U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\not\preceq U\}\right)}{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m(n)}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}.

(4)

Let
b,c\geq 1
be the numbers in Lemma
36
.
Then, by the lemma, for any
n
and
p
such that
\lceil p\log n\rceil\geq b
,
each
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{c\lceil p\log n\rceil}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})
contains at least one
U

that has
S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}
as a subcontext.
Thus, for
m=c\lceil p\log n\rceil
,

\#\!\left(\{U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\not\preceq U\}\right)
is bounded above by:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)-1=(1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)})\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)\leq(1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right).\end{array}

Here
\gamma
is the constant (that only depends on
\Sigma
)
of Lemma
41
, and
r
is the largest arity of
\Sigma
. In
the last inequality, we have used the fact that

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\leq rm}({\Sigma})
.

By using the upper-bound above, Equation (
4
), and
\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})\geq\frac{n}{2rm}

(Lemma
15
),
we have:

\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n}\leq\dfrac{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}(1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m(n)}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}\\
=\dfrac{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}(1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m(n)}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}\\
\leq\dfrac{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m}({\mathcal{G}},N)}(1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})^{\frac{n}{2rm}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}{\sum_{\widetilde{E}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{m(n)}({\mathcal{G}},N)}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathtt{shn}(\widetilde{E})}\#\!\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{m}_{\widetilde{E}{\centerdot}i}({\mathcal{G}})\right)}=(1-\dfrac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})^{\frac{n}{2rm}}\end{array}

for any
n
and
p
such that
\lceil p\log n\rceil\geq b
and
m=c\lceil p\log n\rceil
.

It remains to choose
p
so that

(1-\dfrac{1}{\gamma^{rm}})^{\frac{n}{2rm}}=(1-\dfrac{1}{\gamma^{rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}})^{\frac{n}{2rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}}

converges to
0
.
Let us choose positive real numbers
a
,
p
, and
q
so that

p
and
q
satisfy the following conditions for every
n\geq a
:

\displaystyle{p\log n}

\displaystyle\geq b

(5)

\displaystyle\gamma^{rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}

\displaystyle\leq n^{q}

(6)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle<1.

(7)

For example,
we can choose
a,\,p,\,q
as follows:

\displaystyle a=\max\{\gamma^{b({rc+2})},\gamma^{rc({rc+2})}\}\qquad p=\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{{\scriptstyle(rc+2)\log\gamma}}}\qquad q=\frac{\mathchoice{rc+1}{rc+1}{rc+1}{{\scriptstyle rc+1}}}{\mathchoice{rc+2}{rc+2}{rc+2}{{\scriptstyle rc+2}}}

In fact, condition (
6
) follows from:

\begin{array}[]{l}rc\lceil p\log n\rceil-\log_{\gamma}n^{q}\leq rc(\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}{{\scriptstyle(rc+2)\log\gamma}}}\log n+1)-\frac{\mathchoice{rc+1}{rc+1}{rc+1}{{\scriptstyle rc+1}}}{\mathchoice{rc+2}{rc+2}{rc+2}{{\scriptstyle rc+2}}}\frac{\log n}{\log{\gamma}}\\
=\frac{1}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}(rc\log n+rc(rc+2)\log\gamma-(rc+1)\log n)\\
=\frac{1}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}(rc(rc+2)\log\gamma-\log n)\\
\leq\frac{1}{(rc+2)\log\gamma}(rc(rc+2)\log\gamma-\log a)\leq 0.\\
\end{array}

Thus, for
n\geq a
, we have:

(1-\dfrac{1}{\gamma^{rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}})^{\frac{n}{2rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}}\leq(1-\dfrac{1}{n^{q}})^{\frac{n}{2rc\lceil p\log n\rceil}}\leq\left(\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{3rcp}{3rcp}{3rcp}{{\scriptstyle 3rcp}}}}.

Since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}}=0
,
we have
\limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}Z_{n}=0
as required.

Remark 43
.

In the proof above, we used
the fact that if
0<q<1
then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}}=0.

We also remark that if
q\geq 1
then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathchoice{1}{1}{1}{{\scriptstyle 1}}}{\mathchoice{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{n^{q}}{{\scriptstyle n^{q}}}}\right)^{\frac{\mathchoice{n}{n}{n}{{\scriptstyle n}}}{\mathchoice{\log n}{\log n}{\log n}{{\scriptstyle\log n}}}}=1.

Thus,
p
should be chosen to be sufficiently small so that Equation (
6
) in the proof
holds for some
q<1
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
6
.
We restate the theorem.

Theorem
6

(Parameterized Infinite Monkey Theorem for Regular Tree Languages)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be an unambiguous and
strongly-connected
regular tree grammar
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
,
and
(S_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a family of linear contexts in
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})

such that
|S_{n}|=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then there exists a real constant
p>0
such that
for any
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
the following equation holds:

\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}=1.

Proof 3.25
.

By Lemma
25
, there exists a canonical, unambiguous and essentially
strongly-connected grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
and
a family
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
of subsets
\mathcal{I}_{N}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}

such that

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\biguplus_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}

and
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{N}\triangleq\mathcal{I}_{N}\cap{{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{inf}}=\{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}\mid{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)=\infty\}
.
For any
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)=\infty
and
{\mathcal{G}}
is strongly connected,
we have
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\infty
, and hence

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{N}\neq\emptyset
.
By Lemma
42
, there exists a real constant
p>0
such that, for each

N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{N}
,

\displaystyle\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\right)}=1.

(8)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\ \limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\sum_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \limd\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{N}}\#\!\left(\{T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\mid S_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\sum_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{N}}\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 1

as required; we have used Lemma
5
and
Equation (
8
) in the last step.

4.
Proof of the Main Theorem on
\lambda
-calculus

This section proves our main theorem (Theorem
2
). We first prepare
a regular tree grammar that generates the set of tree representations of
elements of
{\iota}{\xi}
in Section
4.1
, and then
apply Corollary
9
to obtain Theorem
2
,
where
(T_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in the corollary are set to (the tree representations of)
the terms in the introduction that have long
\beta
-reduction sequences.

4.1.
Regular Tree Grammar
{\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
of
{\iota}{\xi}

Recall that
{\iota}{\xi}
is the set of (
\alpha
-equivalence classes of)
closed well-typed terms, whose order, internal arity,
and number of variables are bounded above by
\delta
,
\iota
, and
\xi

(consult Definition
2.1
for the precise definition).
The set
{\iota}{\xi}
can be generated by the following grammar (up to isomorphism).

{defi}
[Grammar of
{\iota}{\xi}
]
Let
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 0
be integers and
X_{\xi}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{\xi}\}
be a subset of
V
.
The regular tree grammar
{\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
is defined as

(\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)})
where:

\displaystyle\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\triangleq

\displaystyle\ \{x\mapsto 0\mid x\in X_{\xi}\}\cup\{@\mapsto 2\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\ \{\lambda\overline{x}^{\tau}\mapsto 1\mid\overline{x}\in\{{\ast}\}\cup X_{\xi},\ \tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta-1,\iota)\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\triangleq

\displaystyle\ \{N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\mid\tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota),\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\subseteq X_{\xi},\ \mathrm{Im}\left(\Gamma\right)\subseteq\mathrm{Types}(\delta-1,\iota),

\displaystyle\ \qquad\qquad\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\triangleq

\displaystyle\ \{N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\tau\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow x_{i}\}\cup\{N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle})\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\ \{N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle})\mid i=\min\{j\mid x_{j}\in X_{\xi}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\}\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\ \{N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow@(N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle},N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle})\mid\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}\}

The grammar above generates the tree representations of elements of
{\iota}{\xi}
,
where a variable
x
, a lambda-abstraction, and an application
are represented respectively as the nullary tree constructor
x
, unary tree constructor

\lambda\overline{x}
, and binary tree constructor
@
.
The nonterminal
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
is used to generate (the tree representations of) the elements of

\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
; the condition

\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\neq\emptyset
on nonterminal
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
ensures that
every nonterminal generates at least one tree.
To guarantee that the grammar generates at most one tree for each

\alpha
-equivalence class
[t]_{\alpha}
, (i) variables are chosen from the fixed set
X_{\xi}
,
and (ii) in the rule for generating a
\lambda
-abstraction, a variable is chosen in a deterministic manner.
Note that
\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,
{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
and
{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
are finite.
The finiteness of
{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
follows from that of

X_{\xi}
,
\mathrm{Types}(\delta-1,\iota)
, and

\{\Gamma\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\subseteq X_{\xi},\ \mathrm{Im}\left(\Gamma\right)\subseteq\mathrm{Types}(\delta-1,\iota)\}
. The finiteness of
{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
also follows
immediately from that of
{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
.

{exa}

Let us consider the case where
\delta=\iota=\xi=1
.
The grammar
{\mathcal{G}}{(1,1,1)}
consists of the following components.

\displaystyle\Sigma{(1,1,1)}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},@,\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}},\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}\}\qquad{\mathcal{N}}{(1,1,1)}=\{N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}{(1,1,1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle})\\
N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle}\longrightarrow x_{1}\mid@(N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};{\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}}\rangle},N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle})\mid@(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle})\\
N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}(N_{\langle\{x_{1}:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle}).\end{cases}

There is an obvious embedding
e^{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
(
e
for short) from trees in

{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)})
into (not necessarily well-typed)
\lambda
-terms.
For
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
we define

\pi^{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\triangleq[-]_{\alpha}\circ e:{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)\rightarrow\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)

where
[-]_{\alpha}
maps a term to its
\alpha
-equivalence class.
We sometimes omit the superscript and/or the subscript and may write just
\pi
for

\pi^{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
.

The following lemma says that

{\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
gives a complete representation system of the
\alpha
-equivalence classes.

Lemma 44
.

For
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 0
,

\ \pi^{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}:{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)\rightarrow\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
is a size-preserving bijection.

Proof 4.1
.

It is trivial that the image of
\pi_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
is contained
in
\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
and
\pi
preserves the size.

The injectivity,
i.e.,
e(T)\sim_{\alpha}e(T^{\prime})
implies
T=T^{\prime}
for
T,T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
,
is shown by induction on
the length of the leftmost rewriting
sequence
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T

and by case analysis of the rewriting rule used in the first step of the reduction sequence.
In the case analysis below, we use the fact that if
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
, then

\mathbf{FV}(e(T))=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)
,
which can be proved by straightforward induction.

•

Case for the rule
N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\tau\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow x_{i}
: In this case,
T=x_{i}
. By the assumption

e(T)\sim_{\alpha}e(T^{\prime})
,
T=T^{\prime}
follows immediately.

•

Case for the rule
N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle})
: In this case,
T=\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(T_{1})
with

N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}
. By the assumption

e(T)\sim_{\alpha}e(T^{\prime})
,
T^{\prime}
must be of the form
\lambda\overline{x}^{\sigma}(T_{1}^{\prime})

where (i)
\overline{x}
does not occur free in
e(T_{1}^{\prime})
, and (ii)
e(T_{1})\sim_{\alpha}e(T_{1}^{\prime})
.
Then
\overline{x}={\ast}
and hence we have

N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle})
with
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}^{\prime}
,
because, if
\overline{x}\neq{\ast}
, we have

N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda\overline{x}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{\overline{x}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle})
with

N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{\overline{x}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}^{\prime}
, and hence

\mathbf{FV}(e(T^{\prime}_{1}))=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma\cup\{\overline{x}:\sigma\})
, which contradicts the condition (i).
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, we have
T_{1}=T_{1}^{\prime}
, which implies
T=T^{\prime}
as required.

•

Case for the rule:
