N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle})
, where
i=\min\{j\mid x_{j}\in X_{\xi}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\}
.
By the assumption

e(T)\sim_{\alpha}e(T^{\prime})
,
T^{\prime}
must be of the form
\lambda x^{\sigma}(T_{1}^{\prime})
where
x
occurs free in
e(T_{1}^{\prime})
,
and
[x^{\prime}/x_{i}]e(T_{1})\sim_{\alpha}[x^{\prime}/x]e(T_{1}^{\prime})
for a fresh variable
x^{\prime}
.
Thus, by the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,

T^{\prime}
must have also been generated by the same rule, i.e.,
T^{\prime}=\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(T_{1}^{\prime})

with
N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}^{\prime}
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have
T_{1}=T_{1}^{\prime}
, which also implies
T=T^{\prime}
as required.

•

Case for the rule:
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow@(N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle},N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle})

where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
.
Then
T=@(T_{1},T_{2})
with
N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}

and
N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{2}
. By the assumption

e(T)\sim_{\alpha}e(T^{\prime})
,
T^{\prime}
must also be of the form
@(T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime})
, with

e(T_{1})\sim_{\alpha}e(T_{1}^{\prime})
and
e(T_{2})\sim_{\alpha}e(T_{2}^{\prime})
.
Therefore,
T^{\prime}
must also have been generated by a rule for applications;
hence

N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{1};\sigma^{\prime}{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{1}^{\prime}
and
N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{2};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}T_{2}^{\prime}
for some

\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
.
By the condition
e(T_{i})\sim_{\alpha}e(T_{i}^{\prime})
,

\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma_{i})=\mathbf{FV}(e(T_{i}))=\mathbf{FV}(e(T_{i}^{\prime}))=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Thus,
since
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
,
we have
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
, which also implies
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
(since the type of a simply-typed
\lambda
-term
is uniquely determined by the term and the type environment).
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, we have
T_{i}=T_{i}^{\prime}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, which implies

T=T^{\prime}
as required.

Next we show the surjectivity, i.e., for any
[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
there exists

T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
such that
e(T)\sim_{\alpha}t
.
For
\delta,\iota,\xi
with
X_{\xi}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{\xi}\}
and
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,
we define a “renaming” function

\rho^{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
(
\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
or
\rho
for short) from
\{t\mid[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\}

to
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
by induction on the size of
t
,
so that
\pi{}(\rho(t))=[t]_{\alpha}
holds.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{\langle\{x:\tau\};\tau\rangle}(x)&\triangleq x\\
\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}(\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}\!.t)&\triangleq\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}(t))\\
\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}(\lambda x^{\sigma}\!.t)&\triangleq\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}(t))\qquad\qquad\hfill(\text{if
}x\notin\mathbf{FV}(t))\\
\rho_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}(\lambda x^{\sigma}\!.t)&\triangleq\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(\rho_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle}([x_{i}\leftrightarrow x]t))\text{ where $i\triangleq\min\{j\mid x_{j}\in X_{\xi}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\}$}\\
&\hfill(\text{if }x\in\mathbf{FV}(t))\\
\rho_{\langle\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2};\tau\rangle}(t_{1}t_{2})&\triangleq@(\rho_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}(t_{1}),\rho_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle}(t_{2}))\hfill(\text{if }\Gamma_{1}\vdash t_{1}:\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\text{ and }\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{2}:\sigma)\end{array}

Here,
[x\leftrightarrow y]t
represents the term obtained by swapping every occurrence of
x
with that of
y
;
for example,
[x\leftrightarrow y](\lambda x.xy)=\lambda y.yx
. Note that in the last clause,
if
\Gamma\vdash t_{1}t_{2}:\tau
, then there exists a unique triple
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\sigma)
such that

\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
,

\Gamma_{1}\vdash t_{1}:\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau
, and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{2}:\sigma
.
We can prove that
\pi{}(\rho(t))=[t]_{\alpha}
holds for every
\lambda
-term
t
such that

[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
,
by straightforward induction on the size of
t
.

Lemma 45
.

For
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 0
,
{\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
is unambiguous.

Proof 4.2
.

The proof is similar to that of the injectivity of Lemma
44
.
We show that, for any
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)})
,
and two leftmost rewriting sequences of the form

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(1)}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{(n)}=T\qquad(n\geq 1)

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{\prime(1)}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{\prime(n^{\prime})}=T\qquad(n^{\prime}\geq 1),

n=n^{\prime}
and
T^{(k)}=T^{\prime(k)}
hold for
1\leq k<n
.
The proof proceeds by induction on
n
, with
case analysis on the rule
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(1)}
.
As in the proof of Lemma
44
,
we use the fact that if
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
, then

\mathbf{FV}(e(T))=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)
.

•

Case for the rule
N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\tau\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow x_{i}
: In this case,
T=x_{i}
and
n=1
.
Since the root of
T^{\prime(1)}
must be
x_{i}
, we have
T^{\prime(1)}=T
and
n^{\prime}=1=n
.

•

Case for the rule
N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau^{\prime}\rangle})
:
In this case,
n\geq 2
,

T^{(k)}=\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(T^{(k)}_{1})
(
k\leq n
),

T=\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(T_{1})
, and we have the following leftmost rewriting sequences:

N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau^{\prime}\rangle}=T^{(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{(n)}_{1}=T_{1}.

Since the root of
T^{\prime(k)}
is the same as that of
T
,

T^{\prime(k)}=\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(T^{\prime(k)}_{1})
for
k\leq n^{\prime}
,
and we have the following leftmost rewriting sequence:

T^{\prime(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{\prime(n^{\prime})}_{1}=T_{1}.

By the definition of rules,
T^{\prime(1)}
must be of the form
\lambda{\ast}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle})

and so
T^{\prime(1)}_{1}=N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
.
By the induction hypothesis for
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(2)}_{1}
,
we have
n=n^{\prime}
and
T^{(k)}_{1}=T^{\prime(k)}_{1}
for
2\leq k<n
.
Thus we have also
T^{(k)}=T^{\prime(k)}
for
1\leq k<n
, as required.

•

Case for the rule:

N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau^{\prime}\rangle})
, where
i=\min\{j\mid x_{j}\in X_{\xi}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma)\}
.
In this case,
n\geq 2
,

T^{(k)}=\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(T^{(k)}_{1})
(
k\leq n
),

T=\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(T_{1})
, and we have the following leftmost rewriting sequences:

N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau^{\prime}\rangle}=T^{(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{(n)}_{1}=T_{1}.

Since the root of
T^{\prime(k)}
are the same as that of
T
,

T^{\prime(k)}=\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(T^{\prime(k)}_{1})
(
k\leq n^{\prime}
),
and we have the leftmost rewriting sequence:

T^{\prime(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{\prime(n^{\prime})}_{1}=T_{1}.

By the definition of rules,

N_{\langle\Gamma;\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma}(T^{\prime(1)}_{1})

implies that
T^{\prime(1)}_{1}=N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau^{\prime}\rangle}
.
Hence by the induction hypothesis for
N_{\langle\Gamma\cup\{x_{i}:\sigma\};\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(2)}_{1}
,
we have
n=n^{\prime}
and
T^{(k)}_{1}=T^{\prime(k)}_{1}
for
2\leq k<n
.
Thus we also have
T^{(k)}=T^{\prime(k)}
for
1\leq k<n
.

•

Case for the rule:
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow@(N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle},N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle})

where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
.
In this case,
n=n_{1}+n_{2}-1
,
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 2
,
T=@(T_{1},T_{2})
,

\displaystyle T^{(k)}

\displaystyle=@(T^{(k)}_{1},N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle})

\displaystyle(1\leq k\leq n_{1})

\displaystyle T^{(n_{1}-1+k)}

\displaystyle=@(T_{1},T^{(k)}_{2})

\displaystyle(1\leq k\leq n_{2}),

and we have the following leftmost rewriting sequences:

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{(n_{1})}_{1}=T_{1}

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle}

\displaystyle=T^{(1)}_{2}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{(n_{2})}_{2}=T_{2}.

Since the root of
T^{\prime(1)}
is
@
,

T^{\prime(1)}
must be of the form
@(N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{1};\sigma^{\prime}{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle},N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{2};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle})

with
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}=\Gamma
.
Also let us consider
T^{\prime(k)}
:
we have
n=n^{\prime}_{1}+n^{\prime}_{2}-1
,
n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}\geq 2
,

\displaystyle T^{\prime(k)}

\displaystyle=@(T^{\prime(k)}_{1},N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{2};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle})

\displaystyle(1\leq k\leq n^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle T^{\prime(n^{\prime}_{1}-1+k)}

\displaystyle=@(T_{1},T^{\prime(k)}_{2})

\displaystyle(1\leq k\leq n^{\prime}_{2}),

and we have the following leftmost rewriting sequences:

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{1};\sigma^{\prime}{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}

\displaystyle=T^{\prime(1)}_{1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{\prime(n^{\prime}_{1})}_{1}=T_{1}

\displaystyle N_{\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{2};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle=T^{\prime(1)}_{2}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow T^{\prime(n^{\prime}_{2})}_{2}=T_{2}.

Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma_{i})=\mathbf{FV}(e(T_{i}))=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}

and
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
,
we have
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
,
which also implies
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
.
Hence by the induction hypothesis for
N_{\langle\Gamma_{1};\sigma{\rightarrow}\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(2)}_{1}

and for
N_{\langle\Gamma_{2};\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow T^{(2)}_{2}
,
we have
n_{i}=n^{\prime}_{i}
and
T^{(k)}_{i}=T^{\prime(k)}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and
2\leq k<n_{i}
.
Thus we also have
T^{(k)}=T^{\prime(k)}
for
1\leq k<n
.

4.2.
Strong Connectivity and Aperiodicity

In this section, we restrict the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
to

{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
by removing unnecessary nonterminals, and
show the strong connectivity and aperiodicity of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

for
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 2
(Lemma
49
below).
Recall that the strong connectivity and aperiodicity is required to apply Corollary
9
and
Remark
10
, respectively.

We define the restricted grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{N\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\mid\text{$N$ is reachable in ${\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}$
from some $N_{\langle\emptyset;\sigma\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}$}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{N\longrightarrow T\in{\mathcal{R}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\mid N\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

\displaystyle\triangleq(\Sigma{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},{\mathcal{R}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}).

For
N\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
, clearly

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)
.
Through the bijection
\pi
,
we can show that, for any
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
also belongs to
{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

if and only if there exists a term in
{\iota}{\xi}
whose type
derivation contains a type judgment of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
.

The strong connectivity of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

follows from the following facts: (i) each
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
is reachable from
some
N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau^{\prime}\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
(by the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
above),
(ii) each
N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
is
reachable from
N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}
(Lemma
46
below),
and (iii)

N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}
is reachable from every
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

(Lemma
47
below).

Lemma 46
.

Let
\delta,\iota\geq 2
and
\xi\geq 1
be integers.
Then for any nonterminal
N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,

N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle}
is reachable from
N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}
.

Proof 4.3
.

Let
\tau=\tau_{1}{\rightarrow}\dots{\rightarrow}\tau_{n}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}
and

\tau_{i}=\tau^{i}_{1}{\rightarrow}\dots{\rightarrow}\tau^{i}_{\ell_{i}}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}

for
i=1,\dots,n
.
For
i=1,\dots,n
, let
T_{\tau_{i}}\triangleq\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{1}}(\dots\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{\ell_{i}}}(x_{1})\dots)
.
Then, we have:

\displaystyle N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{i}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{1}}(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau^{i}_{2}{\rightarrow}\dots{\rightarrow}\tau^{i}_{\ell_{i}}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{1}}(\dots\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{\ell_{i}}}(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\mathtt{o}\rangle})\dots)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{1}}(\dots\lambda*^{\tau^{i}_{\ell_{i}}}(x_{1})\dots)=T_{\tau_{i}}

and hence

\displaystyle N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\phantom{{}^{*}}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\mathtt{o}\rangle})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\phantom{{}^{*}}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(@(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{n}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{n}\rangle}))\longrightarrow^{*}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(@(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{n}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},T_{\tau_{n}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(@({@(\dots@(N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{2}{\rightarrow}\cdots{\rightarrow}\tau_{n}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},T_{\tau_{2}}),\dots)},T_{\tau_{n}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\phantom{{}^{*}}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(@({@(\dots@(@(N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau_{1}{\rightarrow}\tau_{2}{\rightarrow}\cdots{\rightarrow}\tau_{n}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle x_{1}:\mathtt{o};\tau_{1}\rangle}),T_{\tau_{2}}),\dots)},T_{\tau_{n}}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\lambda x_{1}^{\mathtt{o}}(@({@(\dots@(@(N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle},T_{\tau_{1}}),T_{\tau_{2}}),\dots)},T_{\tau_{n}})).

Lemma 47
.

Let
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 2
be integers.
Then for any nonterminal
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,

N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}
is reachable from
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
.

Proof 4.4
.

Suppose
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
.
By the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
(and
{\mathcal{N}}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
), there exists
t
such that

[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
.
Let
T\triangleq\pi^{-1}([t]_{\alpha})\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
.
Now
T
contains at least one (possibly bound) variable, say
x
, and let
\sigma
be the type of
x
.
Since
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left(N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\right)
, there exists a linear context
C
such that

N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}C[N_{\langle\{x:\sigma\};\sigma\rangle}]\longrightarrow C[x]=T.

We show, by the induction on the structure of
\sigma
, that
N_{\langle\{x:\sigma\};\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}C^{\prime}[N_{\langle\{y:\mathtt{o}\};y\rangle}]
for some
y
and linear context
C^{\prime}
.
The case for
\sigma=\mathtt{o}
is obvious. If
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\to\tau^{\prime}
, then since
\xi\geq 2
,
we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{l}N_{\langle\{x:\sigma\};\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}N_{\langle\{x:\sigma,x_{i}:\sigma^{\prime}\};\tau^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(@(N_{\langle\{x:\sigma\};\sigma\rangle},N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\sigma^{\prime}\};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}))\\
\longrightarrow\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(@(x,N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\sigma^{\prime}\};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}))\end{array}

by “
\eta
-expansion”. By the induction hypothesis, we have
N_{\langle\{x_{i}:\sigma^{\prime}\};\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}C^{\prime\prime}[N_{\langle\{y:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle}]
for some
y
and 1-context
C^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus, the result holds for
C^{\prime}=\lambda x_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(@(x,C^{\prime\prime}))
.

By using the property above, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}\longrightarrow^{*}C[N_{\langle\{x:\sigma\};\sigma\rangle}]\longrightarrow^{*}C[C^{\prime}[N_{\langle\{y:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle}]]\\
\longrightarrow C[C^{\prime}[@(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o}\rangle},N_{\langle\{y:\mathtt{o}\};\mathtt{o}\rangle})]]\longrightarrow C[C^{\prime}[@(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}\to\mathtt{o}\rangle},y)]].\end{array}

Thus,
N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}
is reachable from
N_{\langle\Gamma;\tau\rangle}
.

Lemma 48
.

Let
\delta,\iota\geq 2
and
\xi\geq 1
be integers.
Then for any integer
n\geq 5
, the nonterminal
N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}

of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}

satisfies
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\right)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof 4.5
.

For simplicity of the presentation, here we identify trees with terms by
the size-preserving bijection
\pi
.
The proof proceeds by induction on
n
.
For
n=5,6,7
, the following terms

\displaystyle\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}\!.(\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}\!.x)x

\displaystyle\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}\!.(\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}}\!.x)(\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}\!.x)

\displaystyle\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}\!.(\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}}\!.x)\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}\!.\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}\!.x

belong to
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\right)
, respectively.
For
n\geq 8
, by the induction hypothesis, there exists

t\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n-3}\!\left(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\right)
. Thus we have

\lambda x.tx\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left(N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\right)
as required.

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 49
.

{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
is strongly connected and aperiodic for any
\delta,\iota,\xi\geq 2
.

Proof 4.6
.

Strong connectivity follows from Lemmas
46
and
47
,
and the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
(as stated at the beginning of this subsection).
The aperiodicity follows from Lemma
48
and strong connectivity.
(Note that for every nonterminal
N
, there exists a linear context
U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
. Thus, for any
n\geq|U|+5
,

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\supseteq\{U[T]\mid T\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n-|U|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle}\right)\}\neq\emptyset
.)

4.3.
Explosive Terms

In this section, we define a family
(\mathit{Expl}_{n}^{k})_{n}

of
\lambda
-terms, which have long
\beta
-reduction sequences.
They play the role of
(T_{n})_{n}
in
Corollary
9
.

We define a “duplicating term”
\mathit{Dup}\triangleq\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.(\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.\lambda{\ast}^{\mathtt{o}}.x)x\,x
, and
\mathit{Id}\triangleq\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.x
.
For two terms
t
,
t^{\prime}
and integer
n\geq 1
, we define the “
n
-fold
application” operation
\mathop{{\uparrow}^{n}}
by

t\mathop{{\uparrow}^{0}}t^{\prime}\triangleq t^{\prime}
and
t\mathop{{\uparrow}^{n}}t^{\prime}\triangleq t(t\mathop{{\uparrow}^{n-1}}t^{\prime})
.
For an integer
k\geq 2
, we define an order-
k
term

\overline{2}_{k}\triangleq\lambda f^{\tau^{(k-1)}}.\lambda x^{\tau^{(k-2)}}.f(fx)

where
\tau^{(i)}
is defined by
\tau^{(0)}\triangleq\mathtt{o}
and
\tau^{(i+1)}\triangleq\tau^{(i)}{\rightarrow}\tau^{(i)}
.

{defi}

[Explosive Term]
Let
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
be integers.
We define the
explosive term
\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}

by:

\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}\triangleq\lambda x^{\mathtt{o}}.\bigl((\overline{2}_{k}\mathop{{\uparrow}^{m}}\overline{2}_{k-1})\overline{2}_{k-2}\cdots\overline{2}_{2}\,\mathit{Dup}(\mathit{Id}\,x)\bigr).

We state key properties of
\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}
below.

Lemma 50

(Explosive)
.

(1)

\emptyset\vdash\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}:\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}
is derivable.

(2)

\mathtt{ord}(\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k})=k
,

\mathtt{iar}(\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k})=k
and

\#\!\left(\smash{\mathbf{V}(\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k})}\right)=2
.

(3)

|\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}|=8m+8k-2
.

(4)

[\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda(\langle\emptyset;\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)
if
\delta
,
\iota\geq k
and

\xi\geq 2
.

(5)

If a term
t
satisfies
\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}\preceq t
, then
\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k}(m)}
holds.

Proof 4.7
.

First, observe that by the definition of
\overline{2}_{k}
, we have:

|\overline{2}_{k}|=7,\quad\vdash\overline{2}_{k}:\tau^{(k)},\quad\mathtt{ord}(\overline{2}_{k})=k,\quad\mathtt{iar}(\overline{2}_{k})=k.

(
1
) is obvious, and in the derivation of

\emptyset\vdash\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}:\mathtt{o}{\rightarrow}\mathtt{o}
,

\overline{2}_{k}
is
a subterm that has a type of the largest order and internal arity. Thus,
(
2
) follows also immediately from

\mathtt{ord}(\overline{2}_{k})=\mathtt{iar}(\overline{2}_{k})=k
, and

\smash{\mathbf{V}(\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k})}=\{f,x\}
.

(
3
) follows from the following calculation.

\begin{array}[]{ll}|\mathit{Expl}_{m}^{k}|&=1+|\overline{2}_{k}\mathop{{\uparrow}^{m}}\overline{2}_{k-1}|+\Sigma_{i=2}^{k-2}(1+|\overline{2}_{i}|)+(1+|\mathit{Dup}|)+(1+|\mathit{Id}\,x|)\\
&\hfill\mbox{(by $|t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}|=|t_{0}|+\Sigma_{i=1}^{n}(1+|t_{i}|)$)}\\
&=1+((|\overline{2}_{k}|+1)m+|\overline{2}_{k-1}|)+\Sigma_{i=2}^{k-2}(1+|\overline{2}_{i}|)+(1+|\mathit{Dup}|)+(1+|\mathit{Id}\,x|)\\
&=1+(8m+7)+8(k-3)+9+5=8m+8k-2.\end{array}

(
4
) follows from (
1
) and (
2
).

The proof for (
5
) is the same as the corresponding proof in
[
1
]
.

4.4.
Proof of the Main Theorem

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2
.

Proof 4.8

(Proof of Theorem
2
)
.

We apply Corollary
9
with Remark
10
to
the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)}
,
which is unambiguous by Lemma
45
,
and aperiodic and strongly connected by Lemma
49
.
We define
T_{n}\triangleq\pi^{-1}([\mathit{Expl}_{n}^{k}]_{\alpha})
;
then by Lemma
50
(
3
),
we have
|T_{n}|=8n+8k-2=\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Thus, by Corollary
9
(with Remark
10
,
which allows us to replace
\limd_{n\rightarrow\infty}
with

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}
),
there exists
p>0
such that,
for
\mathcal{I}\triangleq\{N_{\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle}\mid\tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota)\}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{(N,T)\in\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)\mid T_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)\right)}=1.

(9)

For
n\in{\rm Nature}
, we have

\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\mathit{Expl}_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}^{k}\preceq t\}\subseteq\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k-1}(n^{p})}\}

(10)

by Lemma
50
(
5
), and

\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\ =\ \,\biguplus_{\mathclap{\tau\in\mathrm{Types}(\delta,\iota)}}\,\Lambda_{n}(\langle\emptyset;\tau\rangle,\delta,\iota,\xi)\ \cong\ \,\coprod_{\mathclap{N\in\mathcal{I}}}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)

(11)

by Lemma
44
.

Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle 1\geq\

\displaystyle\frac{\#\!\left(\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k-1}(n^{p})}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\frac{\#\!\left(\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\mathit{Expl}_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}^{k}\preceq t\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\right)}

(
\because
by (
10
))

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{\#\!\left(\{(N,T)\in\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)\mid T_{\lceil p\log n\rceil}\preceq T\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\coprod_{N\in\mathcal{I}}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\emptyset}{(\delta,\iota,\xi)},N\right)\right)}

(
\because
by (
11
))

Since the right hand side converges to
1
by (
9
), we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\!\left(\{[t]_{\alpha}\in\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\mid\beta(t)\geq{\mathbf{exp}_{k-1}(n^{p})}\}\right)}{\#\!\left(\Lambda_{n}(\delta,\iota,\xi)\right)}=1

as required.

5.
Related Work

As mentioned in Section
1
,
there are several pieces of work on probabilistic properties of
untyped
\lambda
-terms
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
.
David et al.
[
2
]
have shown that almost all untyped

\lambda
-terms are strongly normalizing, whereas the result is opposite
for terms expressed in SK combinators (the latter result has later been generalized for arbitrary Turing complete combinator bases
[
24
]
).
Their former result implies that untyped
\lambda
-terms
do
not
satisfy the infinite monkey theorem, i.e., for any term

t
, the probability that a randomly chosen term of size
n
contains

t
as a subterm tends to
zero
.

Bendkowski et al.
[
4
]
proved that almost all terms in de
Brujin representation are not strongly normalizing, by regarding the
size of an index
i
is
i+1
, instead of the constant
1
.
The discrepancies among those results suggest that this kind of probabilistic property
is quite fragile and depends on the definition of the syntax and the size of terms.
Thus, the setting of our paper, especially the assumption on
the boundedness of internal arities and the number of variables is a matter of debate,
and it would be interesting to study how the result changes for different assumptions.

We are not aware of similar studies on
typed

\lambda
-terms.
In fact, in their paper
about combinatorial aspects of
\lambda
-terms,
Grygiel and Lescanne
[
3
]

pointed out that
the combinatorial study of typed
\lambda
-terms is difficult, due to
the lack of (simple) recursive definition of typed terms.
In the present paper, we have avoided the difficulty
by making the assumption on the boundedness of internal arities and the number
of variables (which is, as mentioned above, subject to a debate though).

Choppy et al.
[
25
]
proposed a method to evaluate
the average number of reduction steps for
a restricted class of term rewriting systems called
regular
rewriting systems.
In our context, the average number of reduction steps is not of much interest;
note that, as the worst-case number of reduction steps
is
k
-fold exponential for order-
k
terms,
the average is also
k
-fold exponential, even if
it were the case that the number of reduction steps is small for almost all terms.

In a larger context, our work may be viewed as an instance of the studies of
average-case complexity
[
26
, Chapter 10]
, which discusses “typical-case
feasibility”. We are not aware of much work on the average-case complexity of
problems with hyper-exponential complexity.

As a result related to our parameterized infinite monkey theorem for trees
(Theorem
6
),
Steyaert and Flajolet
[
27
]
studied the probability that a given pattern
(which is, in our terminology, a tree context of which every leaf is a hole)
occurs at a randomly chosen node of a randomly chosen tree. Their result immediately yields
a
non-parameterized
infinite monkey theorem for trees (which says that
the probability that a given pattern occurs in a sufficiently large tree tends to
1
),
but their technique does not seem directly applicable to obtain our
parameterized
version.

6.
Conclusion

We have shown that almost every simply-typed
\lambda
-term of order
k

has a
\beta
-reduction sequence as long as
(k-1)
-fold exponential in the term size,
under a certain assumption. To our knowledge, this is the first result of this kind for
typed
\lambda
-terms. We obtained the result through the parameterized infinite monkey theorem
for regular tree grammars, which may be of independent interest.

A lot of questions are left for future work, such as (i) whether
our assumption (on the boundedness of arities and the number of
variables) is reasonable, and how the result changes for different assumptions, (ii)
whether our result is optimal (e.g., whether almost every term has a
k
-fold exponentially long reduction sequence), and (iii) whether similar results hold for Terui’s decision
problems
[
15
]
and/or the higher-order model checking problem
[
7
]
.
To resolve the question (ii) above, it may be useful to conduct
experiments to count the number of reduction steps for randomly generated terms.

Appendix B
Proof of Canonical Form Lemma

This section proves Lemma
25
in Section
3.3
.
We describe the required transformation as the composite of two transformations.
In the first step, we transform a grammar to
semi-canonical
one:

{defi}
[Semi-Canonical Grammar]
A rewriting rule is in
semi-canonical form
if it is in canonical form or of the form

N\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}N^{\prime}.

A grammar
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
is
semi-canonical
if every rewriting rule is in semi-canonical form.

Lemma
25
will be shown by the next two lemmas.

Lemma 51
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar that is unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.
Then one can (effectively) construct a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
with
{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is semi-canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.

•

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.

Proof B.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a grammar that satisfies the assumption.
We can assume without loss of generality that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
N_{0}\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

Assume
{\mathcal{G}}
has a rule of the form

N^{\prime}\longrightarrow a(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})\qquad\mbox{($T_{1},\dots,T_{n}$ are $(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})$-trees)}

such that
T_{i}\notin{\mathcal{N}}
for some
i
.
(If
{\mathcal{G}}
does not have a rule of this form, then
{\mathcal{G}}
is already semi-canonical.)
Let
N^{\prime\prime}
be a fresh nonterminal not in
{\mathcal{N}}
.
Consider the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}\cup\{N^{\prime\prime}\},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime})
where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime}=({\mathcal{R}}\;\setminus

\displaystyle\;\{\;N^{\prime}\longrightarrow a(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})\;\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\;\{\;N^{\prime}\longrightarrow a(T_{1},\dots,T_{i-1},N^{\prime\prime},T_{i+1},\dots,T_{n}),\quad N^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow T_{i}\;\}.

Then we can show the following claim by induction on the length of rewriting sequences.

Claim

For every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
and
(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
-tree
T
,

•

N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}T
if and only if
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}T
, and

•

N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}^{*}T
if and only if
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},\ell}^{*}T
.

This claim implies that
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
is unambiguous and
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

We show that
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
is essentially strongly-connected.
Let
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}\cup\{N^{\prime\prime}\}
and assume
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{1}\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{2}\right)\right)=\infty
.
We should show that
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
N_{1}\neq N^{\prime\prime}
and
N_{2}\neq N^{\prime\prime}
by the following argument:

•

If
N_{1}=N^{\prime\prime}
, then
T_{i}
must contain a nonterminal
N_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}
with
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Since
N^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}T_{i}
is the unique rule for
N^{\prime\prime}
and
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}\right)\neq\emptyset
for each nonterminal
N_{0}
appearing in
T_{i}
.
Hence
N^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}T_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}S[N_{1}^{\prime}]
for some linear context
S
over
\Sigma
by rewriting each occurrence of a nonterminal
N_{0}
in
T_{i}
(except for one occurrence of
N_{1}^{\prime}
) with an element of
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{0}\right)
.
So
N_{1}^{\prime}
is reachable from
N^{\prime\prime}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since the reachability relation is transitive, it suffices to show that
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}
, which satisfies
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
.

•

If
N_{2}=N^{\prime\prime}
, then
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
is infinite and
N^{\prime\prime}
is reachable from
N^{\prime}
.
(Here we use the assumption that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
N_{0}\in{\mathcal{N}}
.)
Hence it suffices to show that
N^{\prime}
is reachable from
N_{1}
.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N_{i}\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{i}\right)
(
i=1,2
), both
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\right)
and
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}\right)
are infinite.
Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected, there exists a
1
-context
S
such that
N_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}S[N_{2}]
.
Since
S[N_{2}]
is a
(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}})
-tree, by the above claim,
N_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}S[N_{2}]
and thus
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
.

We show that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime})
.
Suppose that
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
with
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
.
By definition,
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}S[N]
.
By the above claim,
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}S[N]
.
Hence
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N\right)
and
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)={\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
, we have
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
.

By applying the above transformation as much as needed, we obtain a grammar that satisfies the requirements.
What remains is to show the termination of the iterated application of the above transformation.
A termination measure can be given as follows: the measure of a rule
N\longrightarrow C[N_{1},\dots,N_{k}]
is defined as
\max(|C|-1,0)
and the measure of a grammar is the sum of the measures of rules.
The measure is by definition a non-negative integer and decreases by
1
by the above transformation.

Lemma 52
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar that is
semi-canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.
Then one can (effectively) construct a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
and
a family
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
of subsets
\mathcal{I}_{N}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.

•

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\biguplus_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.

Proof B.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a grammar that satisfies the assumption.
Let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
be the subset of
{\mathcal{N}}
defined by

N_{0}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\;\Leftrightarrow\;(N_{0}\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}))\in{\mathcal{R}}\;\;\mbox{for some $a,N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}$}.

The rewriting rule
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}:=\{N_{0}\longrightarrow a(N_{1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}^{\prime})\mid~

\displaystyle N_{0},N^{\prime}_{1},\dots,N^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a)}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},

\displaystyle N_{0}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}a(N^{\prime}_{1},\dots,N^{\prime}_{\Sigma(a)})\}.

In other words,
N_{0}\longrightarrow a(N_{1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)}^{\prime})
is in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a rule
N_{0}\longrightarrow a(N_{1},\dots,N_{\Sigma(a)})
in
{\mathcal{R}}
with
N_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
Given a nonterminal
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{N}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
be the subset given by

\mathcal{I}_{N}\triangleq\{N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\mid N\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}N^{\prime}\}.

We show that the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}\triangleq(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})
together with
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
satisfies the requirement.

We can prove the following claims by induction on the length of rewriting sequences:

(1)

For
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
and
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime})
, if
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}T
, then
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T
for some
N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
.

(2)

For
N^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
and
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma\cup{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime})
, if
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T
, then
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}T
.

We show that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\bigcup_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
.
The above
(1)
shows that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
.
The above
(2)
shows that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)
for every
N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{N}
, we have
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N^{\prime}
and thus
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
for every
N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
.
So
\bigcup_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
.

We prove by contraposition that, for
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
, if
N_{1}\neq N_{2}
, then
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}\right)\cap{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{2}\right)=\emptyset
.
Suppose that
T\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}\right)\cap{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{2}\right)
.
Then we have

N_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}a(N^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,N^{\prime}_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T\qquad(i=1,2)

for some
N^{\prime}_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
.
By the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, we have

N_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{i,1},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}a(N^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,N^{\prime}_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T\qquad(i=1,2).

Because
N_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
, we have
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
By the claim
(2)
, we have

N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{i,1},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}a(N^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,N^{\prime}_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}T\qquad(i=1,2).

The above two rewriting sequences of
{\mathcal{G}}
induce the following two leftmost rewriting sequences:

N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}^{*}N_{i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}a(N_{i,1},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}^{*}T\qquad(i=1,2),

which are the same by the unambiguity of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Then the length of the sequence
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}^{*}N_{1}
and that of
N\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}},\ell}^{*}N_{2}
are the same
since just after them the root terminal
a
occurs, and hence
N_{1}=N_{2}
, as required.

Next we prove the unambiguity of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, by contradiction:
we assume that
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is not unambiguous,
and show that
{\mathcal{G}}
is not unambiguous, which is a contradiction.
Let
N_{0}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
and
T\in{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
be a pair of a nonterminal and a tree such that there exist two leftmost rewriting sequences from
N_{0}^{\prime}
to
T
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that the leftmost rewriting sequences differ on the first step.
Assume that the two rewriting sequences are

N_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\ell}a(N_{1,1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)}^{\prime})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\ell}^{*}a(T_{1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})=T

and

N_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\ell}a(N_{2,1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{2,\Sigma(a)}^{\prime})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},\ell}^{*}a(T_{1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})=T.

We have
N_{1,j}^{\prime}\neq N_{2,j}^{\prime}
for some
j
.
By the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, there are rules

N_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{1,1},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad N_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{2,1},\dots,N_{2,\Sigma(a)})

with
N_{i,j}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{i,j}^{\prime}
for every
(i,j)\in\{1,2\}\times\{1,\dots,\Sigma(a)\}
.
There are two cases:

•

Case
N_{1,j}\neq N_{2,j}
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,\Sigma(a)\}
:
By the rewriting sequences above and the claim
(2)
, we have two sequences

N_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}a(N_{i,1},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(a)})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}a(N_{i,1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(a)}^{\prime})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}a(T_{1},\dots,T_{\Sigma(a)})

for
i=1,2
.
The corresponding leftmost rewriting sequences differ at the first step.
So
{\mathcal{G}}
is unambiguous.

•

Case
N_{1,j}=N_{2,j}
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,\Sigma(a)\}
:
Let
j
be an index such that
N_{1,j}^{\prime}\neq N_{2,j}^{\prime}
and
N_{j}\triangleq N_{1,j}=N_{2,j}
.
Then
N_{j}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{1,j}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T_{j}
and
N_{j}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{2,j}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}T_{j}
.
By the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
and the claim
(2)
, we have

N_{j}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{i,j}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}b(N_{i,1}^{\prime\prime},\dots,N_{i,\Sigma(b)}^{\prime\prime})\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}T_{j}\qquad(i=1,2)

with
N_{1,j}^{\prime}\neq N_{2,j}^{\prime}
.
Hence
{\mathcal{G}}
is not unambiguous.

We show that
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is essentially strongly-connected.
Let
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{2}\right)\right)=\infty
.
We show that
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
.
By the claim
(2)
,
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}\right)\supseteq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{2}\right)
is infinite.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}\right)
is infinite, we have a rule

N_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}a(N_{1,1},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)})

in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1,i}\right)
is infinite for some
i
and
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1,j}\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
j=1,\dots,\Sigma(a)
.
Again, by the claim
(2)
,
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1,i}\right)\supseteq{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1,i}\right)
is infinite.
Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected, there exists a linear context
S
such that
N_{1,i}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}S[N_{2}]
.
By the claim
(1)
, there exists
N_{1,i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N_{1,i}}
such that
N_{1,i}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}S[N_{2}]
.
Since
N^{\prime}_{1,i}\in\mathcal{I}_{N_{1,i}}
, we have
N_{1,i}\longrightarrow^{*}_{{\mathcal{G}}}N^{\prime}_{1,i}
, and hence
by the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, we find that

N_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}a(N_{1,1},\dots,N_{1,i-1},N_{1,i}^{\prime},N_{1,i+1},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)})

is in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
.
Let us pick
T_{1,j}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1,j}\right)
for each
j\neq i
; then we have:

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}a(N_{1,1},\dots,N_{1,i-1},N_{1,i}^{\prime},N_{1,i+1},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}a(N_{1,1},\dots,N_{1,i-1},S[N_{2}],N_{1,i+1},\dots,N_{1,\Sigma(a)})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}a(T_{1,1},\dots,T_{1,i-1},S[N_{2}],T_{1,i+1},\dots,T_{1,\Sigma(a)}).

Thus
N_{2}
is reachable from
N_{1}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
.

Lastly, we show that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.
Assume that
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
and
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\right)\right)=\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}\right)=\bigcup_{N_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N_{1}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}\right)
, there exists
N_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N_{1}}
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Since
N_{2}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}S[N_{1}]
and
N_{1}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}N_{1}^{\prime}
, we have
N_{2}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{*}S[N_{1}^{\prime}]
.
By the claim
(1)
, there exists
N_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N_{2}}
such that
N_{2}^{\prime}\longrightarrow_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}^{*}S[N_{1}^{\prime}]
.
Hence
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}^{\prime}\right)
.
Since
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{1}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
, we have
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\infty
as well.

Proof B.3

(Proof of Lemma
25
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a regular tree grammar that is unambiguous and strongly connected.
Then
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected.
So by applying Lemmas
51
and
52
, we obtain a grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
and a family
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is canonical, unambiguous and essentially strongly-connected.

•

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)=\biguplus_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime},N^{\prime}\right)
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.

The grammar
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
and a family
(\mathcal{I}_{N})_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}
satisfy the first and second requirements of Lemma
25
.
We show that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
implies
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
.

Suppose that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)=\bigcup_{N\in{\mathcal{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)
and
{\mathcal{N}}
is finite, there exists
N_{0}\in{\mathcal{N}}
such that
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{0}\right)\right)=\infty
.
By strong connectivity of
{\mathcal{G}}
, we have
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N\right)\right)=\infty
for every
N\in{\mathcal{N}}
.
Hence
{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}={\mathcal{N}}
, and thus
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})
.
We have
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inf}}({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime})
as required.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
37

We start from the formal definition of periodicity and its basic properties.

{defi}
[Period, Basic Period]
Let
X\subseteq{\rm Nature}
be a subset of natural numbers.
We say
X
is
periodic
with
period
c
(
c\in{\rm Nature}\backslash\{0\}
) if

\exists n_{0}\in{\rm Nature}.\;\forall n\geq n_{0}.\;\forall k\in{\rm Nature}.\quad n\in X\Leftrightarrow(n+ck)\in X.

If
X
is periodic, we call the minimum period the
basic period
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a regular tree grammar and
\kappa=(N_{1}\dots N_{k}{\Rightarrow}N)
be a context type of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
We say
\kappa
is
periodic with period
c
if so is the subset of natural numbers

\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},\kappa\right)\neq\emptyset\},

and call the minimum period the
basic period
.

Lemma 53
.

Let
X\subseteq{\rm Nature}
be a subset of natural numbers and
c\in{\rm Nature}\backslash\{0\}
.

(1)

Suppose that

\exists n_{0}\in{\rm Nature}.\;\forall n\geq n_{0}.\quad n\in X\Rightarrow n+c\in X.

Then
X
is periodic with period
c
.

(2)

Suppose that

\exists n_{0}\in{\rm Nature}.\;\exists k_{0}\in{\rm Nature}.\;\forall n\geq n_{0}.\;\forall k\geq k_{0}.\quad n\in X\Rightarrow n+ck\in X.

Then
X
is periodic with period
c
.

(3)

Suppose that
c
and
c^{\prime}
are periods of
X
.
Then
\gcd(c,c^{\prime})
is also a period of
X
, where
\gcd(c,c^{\prime})
is the greatest common divisor.

Proof C.1
.

(
1
)
Let
n_{0}\in{\rm Nature}
be a witness of the assumption.
Then for every
n\geq n_{0}
and
k\geq 0
, we have
n\in X\Rightarrow(n+ck)\in X
, by induction on
k
.
Let
Y\subseteq{\rm Nature}
be the set of counterexamples of
n+c\in X\Rightarrow n\in X
, namely,

Y\triangleq\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid n+c\in X\;\wedge\;n\notin X\}.

Then
Y
has at most
n_{0}+c
elements because, for every
n\geq n_{0}
and
k\geq 1
,

n\in Y\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+c\in X\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+ck\in X\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+ck\notin Y.

Let
n_{0}^{\prime}\geq n_{0}
be a natural number that is (strictly) greater than all elements of
Y
.
Then for every
n\geq n_{0}^{\prime}
,

n\in X\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad n+c\in X.

By induction on
k\geq 0
, we conclude that
n\in X\Leftrightarrow n+kd\in X
for every
n\geq n_{0}^{\prime}
.

(
2
)
Let
n_{0}
and
k_{0}
be witnesses of the assumption and
Y\subseteq{\rm Nature}
be the set of counterexamples of
n\in X\Rightarrow n+c\in X
, i.e.,

Y\triangleq\{n\in{\rm Nature}\mid n\in X\;\wedge\;n+c\notin X\}.

Then
Y
has at most
n_{0}+ck_{0}
elements because, for every
n\geq n_{0}
and
k\geq k_{0}
,

n\in Y\quad\Rightarrow\quad n\in X\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+c(k+1)\in X\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+ck\notin Y.

Let
n_{0}^{\prime}
be a natural number that is (strictly) greater than all elements of
Y
.
Then for every
n\geq n_{0}^{\prime}
,

n\in X\quad\Rightarrow\quad n+c\in X.

Hence by item (
1
),
X
is periodic with period
c
.

(
3
)
The claim trivially holds if
\gcd(c,c^{\prime})=c
or
c^{\prime}
.
We assume that
\gcd(c,c^{\prime})<c,c^{\prime}
.
Let
d=\gcd(c,c^{\prime})
.
There exist integers
a,b
such that
ac+bc^{\prime}=d
(by Bézout’s lemma).
Since
0<d<c,c^{\prime}
, exactly one of
a
and
b
is negative.
Suppose that
a>0
and
b<0
.

Let
n_{0}
be a constant such that

\forall n\geq n_{0}.\;\forall k\in{\rm Nature}.\quad n\in X\Leftrightarrow(n+ck)\in X

and
n_{0}^{\prime}
be a constant for a similar condition for
c^{\prime}
.
Let
n_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\max(n_{0},n_{0}^{\prime})-bc^{\prime}
.
Then, for every
n\geq n_{0}^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle n\in X

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad n+bc^{\prime}\in X

(since
n+bc^{\prime}\geq n_{0}^{\prime}
and
b<0
)

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad n+bc^{\prime}+ac\in X

(since
n+bc^{\prime}\geq n_{0}
and
a>0
)

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad n+d\in X.

By induction on
k\geq 0
, we conclude that
n\in X\Leftrightarrow n+kd\in X
for every
n\geq n_{0}^{\prime\prime}
.

We show that
N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
is periodic for every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and its basic period is independent of the choice of
N
and
N^{\prime}
.
We call the basic period of
N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
, which is independent of
N
and
N^{\prime}
, the
basic period of the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Further we show that
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
implies
|U|\equiv|U^{\prime}|\mod c
, where
c
is the basic period of the grammar
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Lemma 54
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}},{\mathcal{R}})
be a regular tree grammar.
Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is essentially strongly-connected and
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.

(1)

{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
is infinite for every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.

(2)

N{\Rightarrow}N
is periodic for every
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.

(3)

Let
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and
c_{N}
be the basic period of
N{\Rightarrow}N
.
Then
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
implies
n\equiv 0\mod c_{N}
.

(4)

The basic period
c_{N}
of
N{\Rightarrow}N
is independent of
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
. Let
c=c_{N}
.

(5)

For every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
, if
U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
, then
|U_{1}|\equiv|U_{2}|\mod c
.

(6)

For every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
,
N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
has the basic period
c
.

Proof C.2
.

(
1
)
There exist
N_{1},N_{2}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
contains a context of size
greater than
0
, because there exists
N_{2}\in{\mathcal{N}}
with
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}\right)=\infty
due to
\#\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)=\infty
.
Let
U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
be a context with
|U|>0
.
Let
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
By essential strong-connectivity there exist
S_{N}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)
and
S^{\prime}_{N}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
, and then
U_{N}\triangleq S^{\prime}_{N}[U[S_{N}]]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
with
|U_{N}|>0
.
Let
U^{n}_{N}
be the context defined by
U^{0}_{N}=[\,]
and
U^{n+1}_{N}=U_{N}[U_{N}^{n}]
.
Then
U^{n}_{N}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
for every
n
and
|U^{n}|\geq n
; hence
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
is an infinite set.
Let
N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
By essential strong-connectivity there exists
S_{N,N^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
, which implies
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
is also infinite.

(
2
)
Let
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
By (
1
),
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
is an infinite set.
In particular, there exists
U_{N}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
with
|U_{N}|>0
.
Let
n\in{\rm Nature}
and assume that
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
.
Then
U\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
for some
U
and thus
U[U_{N}]\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n+|U_{N}|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
Therefore
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
implies
{\mathcal{L}}_{n+|U_{N}|}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
.
By applying Lemma
53
(
1
),
N{\Rightarrow}N
is periodic with period
|U_{N}|
.

(
3
)
Let
N\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and
c_{N}
be the basic period of
N{\Rightarrow}N
,
and suppose that
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
.
If
n=0
, then the claim trivially holds.
Suppose that
n>0
and let
U\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
Then by the argument of the proof of (
1
),
N{\Rightarrow}N
has period
|U|=n
.
By Lemma
53
(
3
),
N{\Rightarrow}N
has period
\gcd(c_{N},n)
, which is smaller than or equal to
c_{N}
.
Since
c_{N}
is basic, this implies
c_{N}=\gcd(c_{N},n)
, i.e.,
n\equiv 0\mod c_{N}
.

(
4
)
Let
N_{1},N_{2}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be the basic periods of
N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}
and of
N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}
, respectively.
We show that
c_{1}\equiv 0\mod c_{2}
.
By essential strong-connectivity, there exist
S_{1,2}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
and
S_{2,1}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)
is infinite (by (
1
)) and
N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}
has period
c_{1}
, there exist contexts
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{1}{\Rightarrow}N_{1}\right)
such that
|U^{\prime}|=|U|+c_{1}
.
Let
n=|S_{1,2}[U[S_{2,1}]]|
and
n^{\prime}=|S_{1,2}[U^{\prime}[S_{2,1}]]|
; then
n^{\prime}=n+c_{1}
.
Then both
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n^{\prime}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N_{2}{\Rightarrow}N_{2}\right)
are non-empty.
Hence
n\equiv 0\mod c_{2}
and
n^{\prime}\equiv 0\mod c_{2}
by (
3
).
Therefore
c_{1}\equiv n^{\prime}-n\equiv 0\mod c_{2}
.

(
5
)
Let
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
and assume that
U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
By essential strong-connectivity, there exists
S\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N^{\prime}{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
Then
S[U_{1}],S[U_{2}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
By (
3
),

|S|+|U_{1}|\equiv|S[U_{1}]|\equiv|S[U_{2}]|\equiv|S|+|U_{2}|\mod c.

Hence
|U_{1}|\equiv|U_{2}|\mod c
.

(
6
)
Let
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
Recall that
c
is the basic period of
N{\Rightarrow}N
.
Since
c
is a period of
N{\Rightarrow}N
and by (
3
), there exists
k_{0}\geq 0
such that, for every
k\geq k_{0}
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{ck}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)\neq\emptyset
.
Let
(U_{k})_{k\geq k_{0}}
be a family such that
U_{k}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{ck}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N\right)
.
Now
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
implies
{\mathcal{L}}_{n+ck}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
for every
k\geq k_{0}
, since
U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
implies
U^{\prime}[U_{k}]\in{\mathcal{L}}_{n+ck}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
By Lemma
53
(
2
),
N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
has period
c
.

Conversely assume that
N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}
has period
c^{\prime}
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
is an infinite set by (
1
), we have
U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
such that
|U_{2}|=|U_{1}|+c^{\prime}
.
By (
5
), we have
|U_{1}|\equiv|U_{2}|\mod c
, which implies
c^{\prime}\equiv 0\mod c
.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
37
.

Proof C.3

(Proof of Lemma
37
)
.

Let
c
be the basic period of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
We first define
d_{N,N^{\prime}}
for each
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
is infinite (Lemma
54
(
1
)), there exists
U_{N,N^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
.
Let
d_{N,N^{\prime}}
be the unique natural number
0\leq d_{N,N^{\prime}}<c
such that
|U_{N,N^{\prime}}|\equiv d_{N,N^{\prime}}\mod c
.
This definition of
d_{N,N^{\prime}}
does not depend on the choice of
U_{N,N^{\prime}}
, by Lemma
54
(
5
).

We define
n_{0}
.
For every
N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}
, since
c
is a period of
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
,
there exists
n_{0}^{N,N^{\prime}}
such that:

\forall n\geq n_{0}^{N,N^{\prime}}.\;\forall k\in{\rm Nature}.\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\mathcal{L}}_{n+ck}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset.

Let
n_{0}\triangleq\max\{n_{0}^{N,N^{\prime}}\mid N,N^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}\}
.

Then:

•

(
1
) follows from Lemma
54
(
5
).

•

(
2
) follows from the definition of
n_{0}
, infinity of
{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)

(Lemma
54
(
1
)), and Lemma
54
(
5
)
(given
n\geq n_{0}
, pick
U\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime}\right)
such that
|U|\geq n
; then
|U|=n+ck
for
some
k\geq 0
).

•

(
3
) follows from Lemma
54
(
3
).

•

(
4
) follows from
U_{N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}}[U_{N,N^{\prime}}]\in{\mathcal{L}}\!\left({\mathcal{G}},N{\Rightarrow}N^{\prime\prime}\right)
and
Lemma
54
(
5
).
